She answered that the four minutes is a fact and not a price, a price being something somebody else pays, and that she had paid it and was calling it a price so that she would be able to tell afterwards whether she had been right. A man of about thirty-one in the back disagreed and was not improved upon.** A man of thirty-four who works nights came in at about two on his way to work and said somebody at his building had given him a night of door-opening work on a sheet of about nine addresses, and that he had said yes to a person he did not know, and had done it before and had not been asked to before, and that he was telling about nine people in a cold room and not telling his own employer and did not know why. **Nobody died, said once and said to be true, and a woman of about thirty-four with a district jacket who was on the road at half past four said afterwards that in about nine years of that job she has never been able to say that sentence and mean it, that on this occasion she could say it and could not enjoy it, and that she has thought about the two not fitting together every day since.** The man of twenty-two ran no piece, was asked twice for a decision, at about half past two and at about five, and gave the same seven words both times — ask whoever is running that piece — and both were obeyed, the first time by a woman of twenty-eight with two children and the second by a man of about sixty with a dog. He has not been asked about either. **The tally of cups in two days, Thursday and Friday, was four hundred and eleven, and the cook of twenty-seven refused the reading of it as a headcount and refused, in the same breath, to be coy about it, on the ground that it is the best thing that room has ever done, and said that once and would not say it again.**

### Chapter 0247 — "Nine Words, And Then A Kettle"

**The Monday morning of week eighty-five, fourteen days into an unheated fortnight, on the morning the supply was due back, in that room, and on a service road in the dark. Load-book entry 250, and its own opening label says this entry is one nine-word sentence, one telephone call, and a fortnight.** Ten on the docket and ten in date, and the fire door in Nine Bridges came up a sixth time and he went at about eight for the first time since the water went down and adjusted it again and expects it back. **A landlord's message on the Tuesday had said the supply would be restored when the water went down; the water went down on the Friday; nothing was restored. A man of thirty-eight in a district jacket said there was a form for it and nobody in that room wanted a form, and a cook of twenty-seven said she had asked three times, had been told three times that the supply is restored when the water goes down, and is not asking a fourth.** About eleven people came between half past seven and about eleven on the first working morning after a flood and none of them came with a technique. **A woman of about thirty-four with a district jacket and a clipboard, thanked by nobody, telephoned at about ten past nine from that counter a man of fifty-one named Amiss, who has stood on a page of about forty-one people since the Tuesday of week thirty-eight and is the one of that page that was never rung, and established and stated once: that he had left his flat at about seven on the Friday with a bag and a neighbour's dog, reached a sister's in another district, was not hurt, is fifty-one with a bad chest, and did not answer a door for two days. She also established that the call sits in about nine minutes of a log nobody has read, that the page exists because a form was put in about four years ago, that it cannot be closed from the outside, that she could have rung him on the Friday and did not because the number in front of her was a man's list of buildings and not a page, and that by the Tuesday it was not useful; that the page is the most useful object in this city that week and the one thing that cannot be used; that there is no job in this city whose work it is to ring people; that about four rooms full of people in this year could have held that job and none of them did; and that she is not going to be coy about any of it in that room.** A man of twenty-two was in that room at about eleven with no docket on a Monday. He had been asked for a decision twice on the Friday, both times obeyed, and he said he had had a whole weekend on about seven words and that nobody had made a decision for him and that he could not make the success of it into anything. **A first-year of nineteen, who had been listening since half past nine and had said nothing, came round the chairs, stood in front of him, and said in about nine seconds: nobody should have to find you to stop me.** He said nothing for about nine seconds, refused when somebody else asked her to say it again, said he had nothing and that this is the first time in about two volumes that a person has said the thing he has been saying at length, and asked for about nine seconds and then for everybody to stop looking at him. **She then gave her own half: that he is not the central owner of anything, that she heard him refuse that on the Monday and has not stopped thinking about it, that he is not on the four pieces and is not one of the four who can be stopped because he is not running one, and that this is the entire reason he has to go; that about four hundred people got a cup of tea out of that room and a cup of tea has a person in front of it and that person can be asked to stop; and that she and he are the two ends of a sentence they both wrote and neither can ask to stop.** He asked for the practical part rather than the personal one and got it: on a Thursday when that telephone rings and he picks it up, he says that whoever is running the piece should be asked, and if nobody is running it he says that nobody is running it, and that this is a true sentence about nine people have now said and that has never been printed. He said it was a good answer, that he was not going to be angry with her about the day and a half, and that he would have taken a fortnight. She said to be angry about the day and a half and not about the seven words. **Then she went and washed up, which is the same work and the same room, and the gas ring was on because she had put it on, and the kettle was on because a cook of twenty-seven had put it on for a cup of tea for a woman of about thirty in the chair nearest the door who has attended on every working day since the Friday of week sixty-nine, and nobody has said anything to that woman in a fortnight and nobody was going to, and a cook put a cup of tea in front of her and said nothing at all.** A cook of twenty-seven said the whole of what she had to say about the heating in nine words: she asked three times, is not asking again, and that is a fortnight and nobody has paid for it; and when asked whether she wanted somebody to write that down, said she wanted somebody to write down the other one and nobody ever does. At about half past four the plate rang and a man of twenty-one answered it and told a woman the room was open and where the dry ground was, having no idea that he was repeating what the man of twenty-two had said to a man with a dog on the Friday, and nobody in that room heard him.

### Chapter 0248 — "Thirteen Lines"

**The Thursday afternoon of week eighty-six, twenty-four days into an unheated fortnight nobody paid for. Load-book entry 251, and its own opening label says this entry is one list, no minutes, a thirteenth line, and a thumb.** The room had refused three requests to be put on a board in a corridor in three buildings in about nine seconds each, and a person of thirty-four who buys training said in the middle of the second refusal that the refusals were the reason the room could now be found by people who can act, which was on the record from the Monday of week eighty-two and was not improved on. **Two other rooms had asked to do what this room does, one in a hall with a hatch in it and one on a Tuesday, and nobody told this room about either until a woman of about thirty-four with a district jacket said it in about nine words and then gave the rest: that neither of them can be taught by anybody in this case, that the only thing on offer is the answer, and that there is nothing else in the room to give them.** Nine people came in with a list of what that room would not do, in the order it had happened. **A man of about fifty-two in a plain coat proposed minutes, on the ground that a decision nobody can read back is a mood, and was refused in about nine seconds: nothing is decided in that room, nine things are said, about nine objections are taken, at four o'clock everybody stands up, and no record of any of it will exist — and he was told that in advance, deliberately, so that the absence of a record could not afterwards be attributed to surprise. He said that was fair, and did not ask again, and is not thanked, and a person with an ordinary coat is not a post.** A man of twenty-nine who works on lifts spoke for about four minutes and named what he could not, which is about eleven buildings, on the ground that in about four years somebody will use a list and a list of buildings is a list of people, and that he has a page of his own in a ring binder and has decided that is the most he will be on. **A cook of twenty-seven spoke for about nine seconds: her piece is a kettle, a chair, a gas ring that came with the unit and about nine hundred cups; the room with the hatch has asked her for the numbers and she has given them; the four hundred and eleven are not hers and were not given; if anybody rings her for that figure she will say it belonged to a woman of about thirty-four with a district jacket who was in a car and had not slept; and if the other room needs a figure it will count its own, wrongly, in the correct order of wrong, which she prefers to handing over.** At about three somebody asked what a person was supposed to do in a room like this if they came in with nothing, and a first-year of nineteen got up, counted the lines on the hardboard, said she was going to write a thirteenth and would say the cost out loud first. **The thirteenth line is thirteen words in her hand: that a person may say they were never here and the room will not argue.** Her stated cost, before the mark was made: that it is the first thing she has written in that room that concerns a person merely being in it, that it is on a surface anybody may photograph, that she has been on about eleven hundred leaflets and knows what a sentence on a surface does to a person in about four years, and that she wrote it without asking the person it is mostly about, having done the same thing once before at a market on a stall-holder's licence, and that the two are the same size. **A woman of about thirty, in the chair nearest the door, who has not been asked anything by anybody since the Monday of week seventy, said one sentence standing to about nine people: you did not ask me. A first-year of nineteen said no. She said that was the correct answer and that she was going to say it anyway, because if nobody in that room says it then in about four years there will be a line on that wall about a person who sits in that chair, and it will be a good line, and she will not have been asked and will not be able to tell anybody she was there. Nobody apologised to her, which was correct, and a cook of twenty-seven said out loud from a sink that an apology would have been a second question. The first-year then asked her once whether she wanted the line kept, and the answer given once was that they are not allowed to keep asking, that this is not a refusal, and that this is the line. She was not asked again and nothing has been asked of her since.** At about half past three the cook of twenty-seven took her own thumb to the board over the door and in about four seconds, in front of about nine people, removed the eight words she had written herself on the Thursday of week eighty-two, which had been untrue for twenty-four days, and left the four words in a sign-writer's hand beneath and replaced nothing. **Her grounds, given: that she wrote them, that she knew they were untrue the morning after and said so in that room and was not made to take them down, and that a hand-written line is removable by its writer and by nobody else; and she asked that it be recorded as taking about four seconds and not about a term. Asked what was to be done about the sign-writer's four, she said nothing, on the ground that the man was about sixty, was paid four pounds, has not been thanked, is not a character in that room and is not going to be, and that the four words are true.** There was a public lesson in the same week and he was not in it: the man of twenty-nine and the cook of twenty-seven gave about forty minutes to about nine people on the Wednesday, nothing shown worked, four of the nine were asked to do it badly on purpose, and nothing was written down. **He telephoned once at about half past two to ask whether it was happening, was told it was, asked whether it had worked, was told it had not finished, and did not telephone again. At about nine she told him in the doorway in about nine seconds, four things in order: that it had been given; by those two and not by her; that she had not been asked to be in it and had not wanted to be; and that two people had asked her how it had gone and she had told both the same thing. He said there was nothing to ask, and was told that he is not permitted to keep asking her that either, and was told by which of the two statements that restriction is.**

### Chapter 0249 — "Four Words In Another Hand"

**The Friday of week eighty-seven, thirty-two days into the unheated fortnight, and the third Friday rota of this movement. Load-book entry 252, and its own opening label says this entry is one thumb, four words, and the number of keys.** Ten on the docket and nine in date; the tenth is a stair fire door in Nine Bridges, the seventh report of the same fault in about a month, which he did not attend and recorded the fault and the non-attendance both, and a district man of thirty-eight read that entry and said nothing, which is the first silence in about five months. **A letting clerk of thirty-nine with a ring file gave the standing sentence about a vacant frontage, the sixth giving, and nobody in that room has ever asked her a question about it, and then volunteered unasked, and not as an answer, that the landlord has not asked about that unit once since the water, that she checked on the Friday and again that morning, that there is nothing in that file about the people in that room, and that if the reason the first year is free is ever needed it will not be in that file and never has been, and that she has said so for about sixteen weeks and will not be asked about it by anybody in this city.** A woman of about sixty-eight, in four attendances in three weeks and silent in all of the other three, including two during the flood when she was given tea and a chair and nothing else, **stood up at about a quarter past four, put her own thumb to the four words in a sign-writer's hand, rubbed, and took about four seconds; a grey ghost of the first word remains and cannot be got off that board; she did not apologise and nobody asked her to. She then wrote four words in chalk in her own ordinary hand: anybody wet, come in. Her stated cost, given before she was asked and in about eleven seconds, addressed to the room: she is sixty-eight; she has been in about four rooms in about nine years where a sign on a door said what a person was allowed to do; in three of them she could not take the sign down and in the fourth she could and did not; that is the whole of her experience and she has not said it to anybody; she did it now because that is the only one of the four signs that was wrong; and she has become a person who can take a sign down, and in about four years somebody will stand in that doorway and ask her to put a different one up, and she will be the one who knows where the chalk is. She added that the four words are true about eleven days a year and false the other three hundred and fifty-four, that a sign does not have to be true all year, and that it has to be true on the day somebody is standing in the rain with nothing, and that she will not say it twice. A cook of twenty-seven, about nine words from a sink: that the new line is four words and hers were eight, that hers was wrong, that the woman of sixty-eight did not say so and she will not say so for her, and that she is going to wash up. The sign-writer is not named, not thanked, and not in that room.** **At about four in the afternoon a woman of twenty-four gave her name at the door as Talia Venn, which is the first time in that room, and about four people heard it and the man of twenty-two did not turn round. Two statements, both standing: that the thing is still able to be taken over, said to a room in which the person most likely to be taken over by is standing with his back to her, prefaced by her own statement that she holds no position in it and has not asked for one, and that it will not be taken by a villain, that there is no villain in it, that there is a company, a district and a woman with a clipboard and all three are right, and that what takes a thing of this kind is a Tuesday, a person who is tired, and a version of it that is obviously better for about nine people, and that nothing in that room can stop any of those three and never could. And that it cannot yet cross a district line, not because of a rule, because there is no rule in this city about a room, and not because of money, since a room in a hall with a hatch in it has been doing it since the flood with a cook's numbers and a man's list of buildings. Asked what is stopping it, she answered: that there is a man of twenty-nine who can get into four hundred buildings, a cook who can put four hundred and eleven cups into wet hands, a first-year who can be asked to stop, and no fourth thing, and that the fourth thing is nine keys, and that there are two.** He turned round and said the count himself, in about nine seconds, for the first time in the volume: **nine are needed and there are two; his cannot be asked back by anybody; the other has been in the pocket of a woman of thirty-one since the Thursday of week eighty-two; that is the entire reason he is not a post; and the other seven do not exist and he has never met a person holding one, and he is saying so in front of the two people who own the other two.** He then asked, out loud, the question that has been on the back of a docket for about fourteen weeks — *who can be asked to take this key back* — of a woman of thirty-one with a diary who came in her own coat and brought nothing: that she can be asked, in about nine seconds, that it has been true since the Thursday it was handed over and will be true on a day he has not chosen, and that if anybody in that room asked her she would have it in about nine seconds without thinking about it, would come in her own coat, and would bring nothing; and that she would mind about nine seconds' worth and then she would be in a coat. **A first-year of nineteen asked whether any of that could be written down and was refused in about four seconds, on the ground that a written answer would put a rule about who may be asked on a wall, that a rule about who may be asked is a rule about who is findable, and that there is no such rule in that room and she is not going to help write one on a wet Tuesday for a reason that will feel like tidiness in about four years. Nine words were identified in that sentence and were not counted by anybody. Nobody wrote anything down and the binder holds seven things and did not gain an eighth.** At about half past five the plate rang and a man of twenty-one answered it, asked the caller nothing, gave her the dry ground, and exchanged two sentences of nothing. Nobody in the room heard them.

### Chapter 0250 — "Forty-Six Lines"

**The Wednesday morning of week eighty-eight at the Exchange, and the evening of the same day in a room off a service road and a corridor. Load-book entry 253, and its own opening label says this entry is one line, one number, and a ring binder off a shelf. IT IS THE LAST ENTRY OF VOLUME 05 AND THE RUN ENDS HERE AT 253.** The eleventh sitting, four weeks to the day after the tenth, in a room whose holder states that it is above the line. **The book opened for about eleven seconds and one line went in and it is the forty-sixth, and nobody was described, nobody was counted, and the number of words was refused and not asked for.** The rule was given unasked and whole for the eleventh time, asked for ten times in nineteen years, and given unasked on two occasions. **The telephone count moved from forty-one to forty-four, three in four weeks, less than the four before and more than the two in the spring; the count has now been announced sixteen times, eleven corresponding to the eleven Wednesdays this book holds and five predating it on a separate sheet she has never shown anybody; and at forty-four calls and forty-six lines the book stands two lines ahead of the telephone and was one ahead four weeks ago, the gap has not closed, and she does not know what makes a gap and has not looked and will not look in front of nine people.** She then named her own delay: the book had been shut for the first eleven minutes of the sitting and about four people had watched her not open it. **Her account: a man telephoned the plate on a door in the Lower Wards on the Friday before the water, was put through to a room of about four people, and was given the address of a hall; he then telephoned that office and asked whether the people who go in that room are people who come there; she told him, as she told the room four weeks earlier, that the question cannot be answered without opening the book in front of nine people, that this is true, and that it is also a way of not doing anything. He came in person on the Friday, said about nine sentences, and asked to be in the book without being asked whether he could or had to; he was not asked to sign and did not offer; she wrote his words in his own words. She said the first such line took four weeks and the second took eleven seconds, and that the first took four weeks because she asked the caller whether she was sure, and that was the act and not the hesitation, and that the condition for writing anything in that book is that the caller said it to her face, which she has never once said aloud in nineteen years and said today.** A young man of twenty-one, in attendance since he was seventeen, reported for the second time that the ninth chair had been moved again by a person not in attendance; she said a chair is a chair, for the fourth time; he said he keeps a count of how often she has said it, that she has never confirmed the count, and that he has never been asked to stop, and was told not to; the chair was against the wall at the end of the morning. **She said one more thing: that she said a thing in that room in the week of seventy-two about a book that grows and what it does to the person who has to hold it, that she has not repeated it, that she is not going to repeat it today when it would have been about nine times easier, and she would like about four people to notice and is not going to explain why, because explaining it is a minute. Asked whether the book has to be carried anywhere: it does. Asked whether there is a room in this city it could be carried to: about nine, she has been to none, she will not be carried anywhere by anybody who has not asked her first, and if that happens the book is a thing being moved, and she has watched that happen about four times in her time in this city, in each case without noticing while it was happening and without anybody else noticing either.** At about half past four, in the room off the service road, **a man of about thirty-one who had not been there before asked for a book of what people had said, and was told it is a ring binder with seven things in it, that it is not a book and not a system and cannot be emptied, that no person who wrote in it can be found in it, and that he may take it off the shelf and read it and put it back, and that if he is the person who wrote one of the seven he may take that page out and nobody will stop him and nobody will be told. Asked who says he is allowed to read it: nobody, come and read it. Asked who is not allowed to know that he read it: that it is a different question, that nobody in that room has an answer, that about nine people have been asked it in the last fortnight, and that there is no paper anywhere in this city that says, and that this was decided in about nine days and nobody in the city agreed to it. He read for about nine minutes standing up, wrote nothing, asked nobody anything, put the binder back, and said one sentence on his way out that about four people heard and nobody wrote down.** **At about half past five, in a corridor, for about four minutes: he said he had been asked to carry the binder somewhere by a woman with a diary who has not been carried anywhere in nineteen years and who has now been asked by a stranger, that he does not think it should go, and that he has thought about it for about an hour; and he was told he had not asked her, that the reason had not been given, and that he has not asked her because she made a statement in that room a fortnight ago that she has not repeated since and he will not be the reason anybody asks her again; and she said that was the first correct thing he had said about it in a fortnight, that she was not going to do anything with it, and that if he tells anybody she said so she will deny it and he will not be able to prove it, and that she has wanted to be able to do that for about a year and it has not come up. She then left into the rain. No scene, no declaration and no kiss; neither said a word about it afterwards to anybody; neither has said it again.** The closing inventory: about eleven people a week are still going to be taught that a release waits for a man in a lanyard and about four of the eleven are on no rota and the number is on the back of a woman's own card and is on no form; about nine people have learned the address of a ring binder; a woman of thirty-one with a diary has been asked to be moved and has not been asked again; a board over a door says in a hand that is not a sign-writer's that anybody wet may come in, which is true about eleven days a year; a hardboard on two nails says thirteen things, of which the first is that the room will not keep anything it cannot take back; a woman of about thirty is in the chair nearest the door and has not been asked anything since the Monday of week seventy and says that anybody who wants to ask her again can come and ask once and she will answer once; nine keys are needed and there are two, one of which cannot be asked back and one of which can be asked back in about nine seconds by a person holding the other; the answers to that question were given aloud and not written down and will not be; and **the heating is still off**.

---

# VOLUME 05 — MOVEMENT IV (Chapters 231–240), "The Deal And The Records" — COMPLETE

**`outline/volume-05.md` governs and it was not re-argued. The load-book run is 234 to 243, continuous, one entry per chapter, no duplicate and no gap, and it does not end here. The ten days are fixed in `workspace/volume-05/batch-0004/PROMPT.md`: one in the last part of week seventy-nine, five in week eighty, two in week eighty-one and three in week eighty-two, with the Exchange on the Wednesday of week eighty and the Friday rota on three of the ten days. No day number, month-name, month-date, day-date or year appears in any of the ten files.**

### Chapter 0231 — "A Man Who Works Nights"

**The Friday evening of week seventy-nine, from about twenty to eight in the morning to about ten at night, at a depot, in a room off a service road in the Lower Wards, and in a repair shop in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 234, and its own opening label says this entry is a man who works nights, two sentences, and a telephone call to a plate.** Eleven on the docket, ten in date, and two of the ten signed off by a district man of thirty-eight who said out loud that he had not called it the fault, that this is the third caller that month to be right about a door closer, and that the other two were also right. **A man of thirty-four who works nights, who has been in that room four times in about seven weeks, has never brought anything, and once turned the kettle plug off after six hours, came in at about half past seven on his way to work and said two sentences he had been holding since the spring: that at about four in the morning he is alone with a pump in a building feeding about nine hundred doors, that at some point in every shift he takes his hand off it and says a thing out loud, that in about nine years nothing has ever happened, and that he has told nobody and came hoping somebody would say something; and that he would like somebody to watch him do it, has wanted that for about two terms and about four hundred times in a corridor, there is nobody to ask, and he is not asking for it that night — and he distinguished the two himself, unprompted, saying that his is a wish to be watched and the one going round this city is a need for a person to be present, that one of the two is about him and the other about a stranger, and that he has never been in a position to tell the difference and neither has anybody in a lanyard.** Nobody improved on him and a cook of twenty-seven said one word that was not agreement. At about half past eight a company telephoned the number on the plate, a letting clerk of thirty-nine with a ring file gave the sentence about a vacant frontage that she has now given to five sets of people, was told it was not about the unit, and said hold on, which is the fourth time she has said it. **What the record has and the clerk does not is that she said the word *vacant* to a company about a unit that has been open every working day for twelve weeks, and asked for no name, because in that office the sentence comes before the name and has done for nine years, and all four calls are on a sheet of paper of her own that she has said three times she will not bring to that room, and nobody asked her about it and nobody thanked her.** A woman of about thirty was in that room when a company was told the unit was vacant.

### Chapter 0232 — "A Man Of Thirty-Seven With A Sheet Of Paper"

**The Monday morning of week eighty, and the same bench at about ten that night in the rain. Load-book entry 235, and its own opening label says this entry is one sheet of a company's paper left face down on a counter.** He administers property for a company that appears in a schedule of title on that row, has held the job for six years, signs the line that moves the money, and has asked twice why that unit's first year is free and been refused twice with the same true sentence — that a lease matter is a ring file in another building. **He said the true thing about money first, unasked, and it was that there is no money in this room: he has gone round a shutter, a counter, nine chairs, a window, a kettle, a bell-push, a piece of hardboard and the floor and found none in any of them; and he has signed the paper saying the first year is free every year for six years and has never been told why.** He then said the part nobody had asked for: that in about four weeks somebody in a building he has never been inside is going to find a unit open twelve weeks and cost nothing, that he will not be the person asked, and that he does not know what he would say. **Two people in the room tried to answer and neither produced an answer, because the only person who knows has said the same true sentence about a frontage five times, is not thanked, has not been asked and was not asked that morning.** The reason he came in person is his own: he signs the money and wanted to have stood in the room before he moved any, and in about nine years he has signed about nine arrangements on that row and been inside one. He left the sheet face down, which is the first object anybody has put on that counter which cannot be picked up and put down again in about nine seconds.

### Chapter 0233 — "Nine Sittings And A Person"

**The Wednesday morning of week eighty, in a back room with the shutters up in another district, and the same bench at about ten with the lamp off. Load-book entry 236, and its own opening label says this entry is a four-column book that stayed shut at its ninth sitting for a reason concerning one individual.** Nine sittings, eight gaps of four weeks, and the run of sittings at which nothing has been added is eight and a half. **The rule was given whole and unimproved: a book of what a caller said cannot be written by the people who did not call. It has been given at all nine sittings and asked for eight times in nineteen years, and the ninth was given to a room that had not asked for it.** Nine chairs, and the ninth turned to face the wall since the previous sitting, put there by a young woman of twenty-one who began coming to that room at fifteen, who has never been asked to move it and has never been named in it, and whom nobody has given a reason; **she is not the first-year of nineteen in the other room and is not that person, and there is one nineteen-year-old in this case and it is the other one.** **The telephone count is thirty-seven, announced unasked, up two in four weeks; she has announced it fourteen times, nine of those corresponding to the nine Wednesdays this book holds, the first five predating it, and she performed that subtraction aloud, refused to repeat it, and said the figure is about her and has never once been about the room, and that the room is older than the book.** One of the two who rang did not come and Bea Nunn has not telephoned her and declined to give her spring reason a fourth time. **The ninth reason that room has given for not opening a book, and the first that is about one person: a woman telephoned in the spring about something she had said, telephoned again to ask whether it had been written down, and cannot be answered without opening the book in front of nine people, which would publish the one thing she asked for. Bea Nunn has not looked, and said that in nineteen years she has answered every question put to her in that room and has one she cannot, and that the question is whether a person she spoke to on a telephone is owed an answer.** A woman of thirty-four who runs a kitchen with about eleven people in it came, was told she could have signed and that the line would be the forty-sixth, and said no, because nobody asked her, and was told she would have said yes, and agreed. **The book is at forty-five lines and nobody signed anything in this chapter, and the volume's figure of forty-six is a later problem and is not this chapter's.** A man of twenty-two sat in a corner for about two hours, said nothing, and was not thanked.

### Chapter 0234 — "Nine Minutes"

**The Thursday evening of week eighty, in that room with about eleven people in it, and the same room at about ten with the shutter up and the lamp off and nobody else in it. Load-book entry 237, and its own opening label says this entry is nine minutes and one figure.** A man of forty-one gave his name, which was Vey, and nobody wrote it down, and he kept his coat on. He said the true thing about money first and unasked — that there is no money in this room, that he has been looking for four months for a thing that does not exist, and that this is the only reason a man of his age is on a service road on a Thursday — and then read one sheet in about nine minutes: one figure and five numbered clauses and no name on it. **Heat, light, water and the two working sockets quarterly from the day the room opened and payable whether or not anybody comes through the door in a quarter; nine chairs and nine more when there are nine who want them, a floor that does not slope, a back door with nothing off its top edge, and a sounder audible from the service road; nothing said in that room to be printed, photographed, described, put on a course or used in any material, in writing and not as a conversation; no person in that room gives a name to anybody, and so no account and no cheque name, the money going into a pool already standing behind about nine hundred doors; nobody from that company in the room more than once a month, on invitation, leaving immediately on request and never again after a second.** And a sixth condition given aloud and not on the page: refusal inside a fortnight, with nothing owed, asked, printed, and no return, supported by one line in his own file which he declined to describe. **A cook of twenty-seven answered the sounder clause first, having put two screws into that sounder herself on a Thursday night with nine people watching and been thanked for it never. A first-year of nineteen then found what is actually wrong with the sheet: money is a name, it is the only name on the sheet and the only one that cannot be refused, a number will be needed to move the money and the number will be that room's, and a number is findable where a leaflet, a photograph and a lease are not. He accepted the description and said he has no answer to it and has had eleven weeks.** Then the argument, better than the terms: that the wall's best sentence has no force, that a rule against being bought is worth nothing to a room that must choose between heat and being bought, that in about four years one of them will need a wage, that the person who finds the wage will own the wall without stealing it, and that a thing paid for is a thing expected, which he has watched four times and paid for twice. **Nobody answered it. A man of twenty-two said the thing that made it worse: he is the only person in this city who can be telephoned about that room, and if his signature moves the money then the first action in that company when the money stops is to ring him, and he would then be the person deciding whether a room off a service road is a room.** A woman of twenty-four warned once that clause three is always circumvented by a route that is not a lie, that she has watched about nine of them and none of those nine was a villain, and that her own has not been asked for and will not be described. Nobody thanked anybody and the sheet lay face up on the counter for the first time in its history.

### Chapter 0235 — "What The Room Can Do With It"

**The Friday evening of week eighty, and a second landing in Nine Bridges at about nine that night. Load-book entry 238, and its own opening label says this entry is one page left face up on a counter for four days and one sheet of job paper that arrived twice.** Ten on the docket, nine in date, and a page that came up twice in one week: a stair fire door whose closer was adjusted in the spring and again midweek, reported twice in a week by two different people into the same box, both accounts true and neither the fault, the fault being about two millimetres of swell on the hinge side of a frame stamped with a number about four feet up. He reset the closer, wrote in the description box that the fault is not the closer, and a district man of thirty-eight said it was the first entry in about four months that had stated what the fault was not. **A cook of twenty-seven then gave the five parts of what that room can do with a page of paper, unasked: it cannot take it, because four names stand on the lease and a fifth on a company's paper is a name that can be looked up; it cannot decline it, because declining is a letter and a letter is a document about the room bearing its occupants' names, which a man of fifty-eight established in that room three weeks earlier; it cannot give it away, because there is nobody to give it to; neither the binder nor the board takes it, each being barred by one of the wall's own two lines; and fifth, the only available act is to leave it visible on a counter and remove it when the shutter comes down — which is the sentence she gave about a pair of shoes about four weeks earlier, and she announced that she has now said it twice and will not say it a third time.** Asha Reed said the consequence out loud: the room will have to decline a page of paper, no declining of a page of paper is other than a paper, that is the first object in about twelve weeks the room has had no way of holding, and she had considered adding a twelfth line to the wall about money for two days and had not, because a line about money written by a person frightened about money is the worst thing that board could carry, and she is spending a man's argument about a pair of shoes about a company. **A man of thirty-four who works nights said his two sentences a second time, having announced the previous Friday that he would, and added that he is not going to ask anybody to act, and that a cook of twenty-seven told him it was not nothing and he replied that it is not nothing and is not a yes, and that he has been able to tell the two apart since about the spring and had told nobody until that night.** A man of twenty-two said one sentence about there being no money and about eleven people a week and said the two facts are in the same building and nothing in this room or this city connects them, and was not asked what he meant.

### Chapter 0236 — "Eleven Lines, Nine Arguments, And One Thumb"

**The Monday afternoon of week eighty-one, in that room with about nine people in it, and the same table at about ten with the shutter still up. Load-book entry 239, and its own opening label says this entry is one sheet of paper with eleven lines and about nine arguments.** The room was ninety-one days old, a first-year of nineteen said so, and a cook of twenty-seven confirmed the figure, said she had counted twice because she counts things, and declined to say what it means, on the ground that she does not know and will not make a coincidence out of it in front of nine people. A cook of twenty-seven brought one sheet off a roll in a kitchen, distinguished it from the wall and from the ring binder, and a first-year of nineteen stated the volume's own definition before a mark was made: **a rule in this building is a line somebody wrote by hand; it costs its writer something said aloud; it binds nobody who did not write it; its own writer may lift it with a thumb; and it is evidence of nothing in any proceeding, there being none.** They wrote for about an hour and forty minutes and about nine of the eleven lines were argued about. **A man of fifty-eight who inspects buses, who is not the man of fifty-eight with the bad hip and was named as not being him out loud while he fetched his pen, wrote that a thing which has failed twice is not a thing anybody should be committed to paper, and said the cost of that sentence out loud as twenty-two years of about nine vehicles a week. A printer of fifty-eight wrote twice, argued that a board cannot be lifted and a drawer can, and conceded that this is not a reason. A cook of twenty-seven wrote a second line at about half past eight and rubbed it off with her own thumb at about a quarter to nine in front of about nine people, on the ground that a line written from annoyance will be wrong by Friday and that a removable line is removed by its own writer, and said aloud that nobody had touched that paper except to write on it. A woman of thirty-one with a key and a diary wrote the eleventh line last, after the sheet had gone round, and signed it separately: that she may be telephoned about any of it and will come and reverse it — the same eleven words she put on a sheet of typing paper about a key a fortnight earlier — and named her own cost unasked, which is that she is now findable about two things instead of one.** Eight names are at the foot. **A man of twenty-two signed and wrote none of the eleven, as he signed the board and wrote none of its twelve, and gave the reason once: that no sentence he writes about that room avoids being one with his name across the front of it, and that one such sentence has been in a rail in Lattice Ward for about a term and cost him the last thing he had.** And a woman of about thirty was in the room for the whole afternoon and the sheet never reached her, the reason announced out loud by a first-year and handed back by the woman in her own words and not improved on: that nobody has put a question to her in about twelve weeks, that nobody is going to begin on a Monday afternoon holding paper, and that she would like to be left in the chair and not made into the thing a person is careful about, and that nobody said that for her. Nobody did. **A first-year of nineteen stated before anybody else could that the sheet is not a constitution and is not to be called a set of rules, and that anybody describing it as either in four years is describing nine people sitting in a room on a Monday afternoon.**

### Chapter 0237 — "What This Room Has Got Wrong"

**The Thursday evening of week eighty-one, in that room with about eleven people in it, and the same room's floor at about ten with the board back on its nails. Load-book entry 240, and its own opening label says this entry is a record spoken aloud in about eleven minutes whose first item is a failure.** The hardboard came off its two nails and went on a chair, and a first-year of nineteen set the terms before anybody sat down: the failures lead, because a record of what a room does right is a document about a room, and about nine of the eleven people present had never been in a room that would say aloud what it had got wrong. **She went first and said so, and it was the four seconds in the week of seventy-two, and she said that everybody had known for about a fortnight and nobody had made her say it, and gave her own price unasked: about eleven hundred leaflets in about eleven hundred pockets with her name in her own hand and no mechanism in this city to remove a name from paper, and about four people in that room who have wanted to ask her something for about two months.** **A printer of fifty-eight read nine words of her own out loud in a room for the first time in her life**, after stating that about nine hundred leaves of a company's pack carry her handwriting at nine inches, that she is the only person who can say with certainty that a printed page cannot be un-printed after being wrong four times in forty years and right once, that the once has cost her sleep and nothing else because nobody telephoned the shop, and that her delay was not modesty but not knowing whether that room would let a woman of fifty-eight hear her own handwriting read by herself. A cook of twenty-seven read eleven items, the eleventh being a woman's name struck through in one unretraced stroke with a biro in about a second and a half before about nine witnesses, none of whom stopped it or was asked to; she refused the name before being asked twice, and said the mark cannot be lifted and the only record of the request will be a smudge, and that she was told at the time she was not the person holding the pen, that this was correct, and that she has turned it over daily for a fortnight. A woman of thirty-one read hers, a man of twenty-nine read nine words and said the price is about four people, a man of fifty-eight with a bad hip read his and corrected his own earlier statement that a pair of shoes was the first thing the room could hand back, **and the man with the docket read nothing and was given nothing.** A woman of twenty-four asked that her three sentences not be repeated, the room agreed in about four seconds, and she named the cost as hers and not the room's. **Then a man of fifty-one with a tool bag said that about eleven people had just been read aloud inside a building carrying a company's number on its outer wall, that he has spent about nine years putting numbers on about nine hundred doors and has never once learned who passes one, and that the room has done in one evening what he has wanted for nine years without anybody making a mistake; asked what it is for, he said a company which can establish a room exists may enter it in a list, and about eleven people a week in this city are already in a list in the same building, and the two lists differ while their membership does not.** The wall was read line by line afterwards, twelve lines, each by its author, and the first line is still the first line.

### Chapter 0238 — "The Same Eleven, And Not The Same List"

**The Monday morning of week eighty-two, in that room, and the same bench at about ten with the tool roll shut beside the lamp. Load-book entry 241, and its own opening label says this entry is one list in a company and about eleven names a week that are not in that room.** The hardboard had been back on its nails for four days, its twelve lines eighteen days old, and the company's page was face up on the counter for the eleventh day. **A letting clerk of thirty-nine took a call and said her one sentence: on the Friday a man from her own floor asked whether the unit was still vacant, was told it was, and then said it was not and that he had been told so by somebody who had read it out of a record; he could give the street and the day and could not give the name of one person in the room; she told him that was the correct state of affairs and that it was hers, being the one who tells him — and then that she is not thanked, will not be, keeps a sheet with four calls on it, and is still not bringing it there.** A person of thirty-four who buys training for a company, who telephoned the plate in the previous month and whose call was followed by a company's withdrawal of a print run, and who is owed nothing and has said so twice, said the useful part first: on the Friday she was asked by a person in her own building to place that room in a list of places, not a document but a board in a corridor, and she declined; the reason is not that she thinks well of the room, but that a company unable to establish that the room exists cannot enter it on anything, and on Thursday night, in a room of about eleven people, the room became establishable, and she was not present and was told afterwards by about four people who were. She then said the half that concerns her own building: about eleven people a week stand on a list there, it is a list of people sent to things and not of places, about eleven a week attend, nobody at that end has ever been in that room, and she has looked at the list and never seen a name from that room on it. Asked whether that is a price, she said it is not, and that the price is that the room is now discoverable by people who can act. **A man of fifty-one with a tool bag then said the two sentences that are his own: that he stands on the list, has for about nine years, entered his own name into its box about four years ago, has never been questioned and has never wanted to be, could take the name out in about four seconds with a telephone, has not and will not, and has thought about it for nine years and done nothing; and that the man with the docket is on it as well, on an exception recorded and renewed for the term, in the same box, and that he knows because he has processed four renewals nobody requested.** Asha Reed said once that the eleven are the same eleven and the list is not the same list, said she cannot prove it, and named the only authority she has: a man of fifty-eight established about a fortnight earlier that a line which cannot be proved is still worth having, and that she is spending it about a room rather than about an object in a plastic bag. **And a man of thirty-four who works nights asked what the heat in that room costs to run, and about four people did not know, and it is the first occasion in fourteen weeks on which the price of the heat and the price of anything else have shared a sentence in that room.**

### Chapter 0239 — "A Key That Comes Back"

**The Thursday evening of week eighty-two, in that room, and the same room's floor at about eleven with his back against a wall. Load-book entry 242, and its own opening label says this entry is one key returned and one key nobody can reach.** The room was a hundred and one days old and the man of twenty-two had no docket, which nobody remarked on. He telephoned a woman of thirty-one at about half past eight, was put through by the clerk of thirty-nine after the sentence about the frontage and a fourth *hold on*, and told her it was about the key; she came at about half past nine with nothing and was thanked by nobody. **He put a cut key on the counter and said the return is not a protest and not connected to the money, and then that a key is only as safe as a telephone, which is the sentence on a sheet of typing paper in a drawer, and that he has let it sit for eleven weeks without acting on it, and that this is the whole of what he has done wrong; that one key cannot be given back because a company is not a person and cannot be asked to take anything back, which is the answer a first-year wrote on the back of a docket about eleven weeks ago that four people gave and nobody recorded; and that two keys in one pocket for eleven weeks is how a list starts, because a person eventually records which pocket, and that in about four years a man with two keys in one pocket where nobody knows all nine is a post.** Then the part that is about the room and not about him: **that nobody has done anything wrong, that the woman of thirty-one's eleven words on a sheet of typing paper are the best thing anybody has ever done for that room, and that he is not handing the key back to her but because of what a key is, and that neither she nor the woman with a ring file nor he is the problem, and that he would like it on paper and knows it cannot go on paper.** A cook of twenty-seven said one word that was neither assent nor refusal and gave its purpose: he has made himself less able to open that door, he did it aloud in front of about eleven people, nobody there will call that a good thing, and she does not believe he did it to be good. The woman of thirty-one took the key at about ten to ten, put it in her own pocket, and said she will not draw up a list, will not write it down, will not say she holds it, and that a ninth holder would have to be the ninth. **A man of thirty-four who works nights asked what the scrap says, was told it is a question one of them wrote on the back of a docket, and said the best sentence in the room is a question, that he has wanted somebody to telephone him for about two terms, that he has just watched a key handed back instead, and that he declines to say which of the two is larger.** Then a cook of twenty-seven said the heat goes off on the Monday, that nobody has ever worked out what it costs to run, that she is not blaming him and will not let anybody else, and that she would like it written down and it cannot be written down. **The first key is untouched, unreachable, unaddressed by anybody except its holder, and nothing was invented and nothing was fixed.**

### Chapter 0240 — "A Fortnight Of Heat"

**The Friday evening of week eighty-two, in that room, and a service road in the dark at about ten at a shutter that is down and cold on the outside. Load-book entry 243, and its own opening label says this entry is one refusal, one fortnight, and eight words above a door.** Nine jobs and nine in date, and a stair fire door whose closer he adjusted on the Wednesday of the week before, again on the Friday and again that evening, which is three times in nine days and which came up a third time with a stamped number in the description box that a district man of thirty-eight had copied off the frame with a step-ladder. **The refusal took about four minutes and about nine people spoke in it.** A first-year of nineteen stated it first: no — not because the terms are poor, since the reader said before reading them that they were the best anybody had put to that room, and not because the money is unwanted, a man of thirty-seven having said on the Monday that there is no money in that room and being right — but because there is nothing on that page any of them can answer, and she had been in that room every day for about fourteen weeks waiting for an answer and had not been looking for one. **A man of twenty-two added two halves: that he declined to read the page as a question about the offer, which is what he had been doing since the previous Friday, and that a first-year told him he had been waiting to be asked his opinion and had not asked, and that the two are one thing; and that he did not vote, that about nine of those present can be relieved of a thing by telephone inside a day, and that he cannot be relieved of that room at all and was not relieved of it that evening.** A cook of twenty-seven put the kettle on and then said, unasked, one figure and then the reason: a fortnight; the heat goes off on Monday; nobody has paid for it and nobody is going to; she has not got the money and it is not hers to find; the only route by which money is found for a room like this is a person who wants something, every version of that has been refused for about fourteen weeks, the heat is what the refusing costs, and she has been putting the kettle on every day for about fourteen weeks while pretending in about nine different ways that she had not noticed. **A man of thirty-seven came for the page in his own coat, did not sit down and did not say he was sorry, and said he had been proved right on the Monday about a thing he was not talking about, that the line in his file will read that the offer was declined, and that he has been told he may not record why and will not.** Then a man of about forty-five stood in the doorway having never been in the room, said he had read about it on a sheet in about eleven hundred pockets, and asked whether it would still be there, and got the **fourth set of nine words in this case**, with the disclaimer made first: **Nobody knows who will be here in a fortnight.** He said it was a no and was told it is not a no, that it is nine words and the truth, and that the truth is not a no. Then somebody looked at the board over the door and there were **eight words above the sign-writer's four, in another hand and another ink — *It is warmer in here than out there* — and nobody had seen them.** A first-year said so out loud and about nine people stood up, and a cook of twenty-seven at a sink said she wrote them on the Thursday night after the room emptied and came back on the Friday morning, and that a line written by hand costs its writer something and binds nobody who did not write it; asked what it cost her, she said the room is warm on a Friday because she has put a kettle on every day for about fourteen weeks, that a kettle does not heat, that it has not heated since the second week, and that about nine people in that room have known it throughout. **And the last sentence anybody said in that room was a first-year of nineteen's, standing: that the board over the door is not the wall and the two are not one object, that the first line on the hardboard is still the first line on the hardboard and is a cook's and is not a rule and binds nobody and is evidence of nothing, that the eight words over the door are not a rule either, and that from Monday they will be the only line in that room that is not true, and she would like everybody to look at it while it is.** Nobody said anything. The shutter came down at about ten, the room was still warm, and it will not be on Monday. **Nobody apologised to the woman of about thirty who has been coming on every working day since the Friday of week sixty-nine and has never been asked why, and she is the person this fortnight is paid for.**

# VOLUME 05 — MOVEMENT III (Chapters 221–230), "Whose Words" — COMPLETE

**`outline/volume-05.md` governs and it was not re-argued. The load-book run is 224 to 233, continuous, one entry per chapter, no duplicate and no gap, and it does not end here. The ten days are fixed in `workspace/volume-05/batch-0003/PROMPT.md`: one day in the second half of week seventy-five, two in the seventy-sixth, one in the seventy-seventh, two in the seventy-eighth and four in the seventy-ninth; the Exchange falls on the Wednesday of week seventy-six and on no other day, and the Friday rota falls on the Friday of week seventy-five and the Friday of week seventy-eight. The eight-word overlap of each entry with its own chapter, measured on the files as they stand with the matcher printed in `workspace/volume-04/batch-0005/SUMMARY.md`, is 59, 13, 39, 27, 34, 29, 33, 42, 13 and 46 — a mean of 33.5 and a range of 13 to 59, which is the lowest mean of any movement in this volume. The batch is 31,651 words (18,633 prose, 13,018 load book), 613 raw instances of *about* at 19.37 per thousand, **468 true hedges at 14.79 on the one reading the printed rule produces — the article in the rule is optional, so the strict and chain branches are a single matcher and there is only one figure** — and 299 bold spans at 9.45 with a worst chapter of 12.00, and the two ceilings in the prompt are bold ceilings and only the bold figure is measured against them, and it is inside both. The hedge matcher reproduces four of the six earlier published hedge figures to the unit and does not reproduce two of them, and this batch's figure is the highest published in this manuscript and is stated rather than dressed up. The backwards eighty-character sweep, run against an index built from all two hundred and twenty earlier chapters, is 66 runs, a largest of 216 and a mean of 103.0, and every one of the sixty-six is inherited refrain, canon the brief requires, the room's own inventory, or the master's blank fifth line. The within-batch pass, all forty-five unordered pairs, is 25 runs, a largest of 122 and a mean of 94.3, against Movement II's 149 / 135 / 94.9. Full log in `workspace/volume-05/batch-0003/SUMMARY.md`.**

### Chapter 0221 — "About Four Hundred People Have Read It"

**The Friday evening of week seventy-five, from about twenty to eight in the morning to about ten at night, at a depot, in four rooms off a service road in the Lower Wards, and in a repair shop in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 224, and its own opening label says this entry is eleven jobs, eight in date, a bin store, and eight printed pages.**

Eleven on the docket, eight in date, and a bin store in a block where a man of thirty-eight in a district jacket has now told three callers in a month that they were right and that it was not the fault. **A district nurse of thirty-four with a district jacket and a clipboard, who put a trestle in a market about five weeks earlier and has not been thanked for that either, laid eight printed pages on the counter: a certificate course in another city, about four hundred readers, and on the inside cover a photograph taken from that room's doorway, showing the shutter up, the nine chairs in a curve, the window and the wall about four feet behind it, the hardboard with four words in a sign-writer's hand, a piece of sandpaper, the ring binder standing open with its pages above the sleeve, and about six unresolvable figures behind the counter.** Asha Reed looked at it for about eleven seconds and said two things, and then said the third: **that she said yes, on a Tuesday about three weeks earlier, in about four seconds, to a woman who had telephoned the number on the plate because she had taken one of about eleven hundred leaflets out of her bag in a market, and that six people were standing in that room when the picture was taken and she asked not one of them.** She named the cost of her own two-year sentence — that nobody in a room has to say anything to be described by the person who has the room — and said she has said it on a floor in week sixty-eight and at a market table in week seventy and has not been careful with it. A cook of twenty-seven said one word from a sink. **A man of twenty-two said that the photograph is not a room described by somebody who was not in it, and was told in front of about nine people that it is a room described by a person who was in it and did not ask six other people, which is worse and is a different thing, and that the person who said it does not know what to do with it.**

### Chapter 0222 — "Eight Sittings And A Print Run"

**The Wednesday morning of week seventy-six, from about half past nine to about half past four in the afternoon, in a back room with the shutters up and then in four feet of shop in the Quiet Quarter, and at ten that night at a bench in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 225, and its own opening label says this entry is a book that did not open at the eighth sitting, and a print run of about nine hundred.**

The back room hot by eleven, the tin into a drawer with no lock on it, eight chairs and a ninth carried in by hand by a young woman of nineteen who has been coming to that room since she was fifteen and has never once been asked to move it. **The rule given whole: a book of what a caller said cannot be written by the people who did not call. Eight sittings, seven gaps of four weeks, seven and a half sittings at which nothing has been added, and the half volunteered for the second time with the fact that nobody in this city can tell her what it means.** The telephone count moved from thirty-three to thirty-five and was announced unasked; she has now said it thirteen times, eight of which are the eight Wednesdays this book holds, and she performed that subtraction aloud and refused to repeat it, and she has not rung the caller who is not coming and has refused her reason a third time in the same words. **The book did not open, and the reason is new: at the seventh sitting it grew, and since then the question asked of her most often is when it will grow again, and she cannot answer it, and a woman who cannot answer the only question there is does not open a book on a table in front of about nine people.** Two items at four minutes each, neither written down. A man of twenty-two sat in a corner for two hours and said nothing and was not thanked. **Then four feet of shop: a printer of fifty-eight and a clerk of forty-four, neither of them in the room and neither thanked, and a page with a photograph on it, and about nine hundred leaflets off one counter in one morning carrying the same picture and the words *our method* beneath it — and the top sheet in that sleeve legible, and the hand on it the printer's own nine words.**

### Chapter 0223 — "Nine Steps That Belong To Somebody Else"

**The Thursday afternoon and evening of week seventy-six, from about half past two to about seven, in four rooms off a service road in the Lower Wards and on the wet service road outside it, and at ten that night. Load-book entry 226, and its own opening label says this entry is nine steps that are not his, a refusal, and a telephone call to a plate with a company's number on it.**

A man of fifty-eight with a hip that did not go right, a bus and one stop too far, and nine steps for getting out of the front seat of a car when one hip will not take weight. **They are his sister's, she wrote them on the back of an envelope in a car park about four years ago because he asked her to, and he said before anybody asked him for it that the words are not his, that he did not learn them, and that the difference between performing a thing and holding it is the whole of that room.** A first-year of nineteen put the binder on the shelf without opening it and gave the reason without walking to the wall: the only person who could take a page back is a woman in another district who has not been asked. **She said the offer was right and the refusal was right in the same breath. He said he had done it wrong twice, the second time on a pavement outside a shop in front of about nine people, and that it therefore qualifies as a technique by that room's own definition, and that he can still remember the wording of the definition from the wall in its first week.** He named the price: four years in a drawer and a sister he has not spoken to in about nine years. **The room then did nothing for four days, which is the instrument it has, and at about seven that evening the sister telephoned the number on a plate with a company's name on it, a letting clerk of thirty-nine gave her the sentence about a vacant frontage, and the clerk said hold on.** A first-year of nineteen took the call standing up; the woman said her brother's words were not hers to give and said the nine steps herself, faster, with a different word for the count, adding two things he had not said — that the chair is not optional, and that where another person is helping the count is not taken by the person being helped. **The page went into the binder as the seventh thing. She announced out loud in front of about nine people that she was about to do the thing a woman of sixty-one's rule is against, was told to go ahead by a cook of twenty-seven, and gave the whole of her justification in about nine words: she said it to a person, and not to a tin — and added, unasked, that she cannot prove the difference and has decided it.** He read it, found a number in it he has asked her for about two hundred times in nine years and has never heard, and said she was wrong, and was told that that is also correct.

### Chapter 0224 — "The Room Is Open And It Costs Somebody A Friend"

**The Monday morning and afternoon of week seventy-seven, from ten past ten to about half past six, in four rooms off a service road in the Lower Wards, and at about half past four in a canteen off a service road. Load-book entry 227, and its own opening label says this entry is a man of thirty-one in the fourth week of a stoppage, an answer that was correct, and the loss of a friendship that had lasted about five hours.**

A man of thirty-one in the fourth week of a stoppage over the removal of a four-minute torch walk-round of the rear of a loaded vehicle, at a depot of about ninety vehicles and about four hundred loaders, who is not a supervisor, has never wanted to be, and said twice unprompted and to nobody that it is not about money. **Nobody in that room asked him which side he was on, nobody asked whether the check should be reinstated, no line of the wall was read at him, and about four people watched a first-year of nineteen decide in front of everybody not to reach for the wall.** A woman of thirty-one with a key and a diary did the door, the sounder and the outside of the shutter with nine people present where she expects four, unaided and unasked, and the shutter was the same in the morning. He asked her one question; they talked for about two hours about a leaflet, a market, a bus, a kitchen and a course; she walked him to a stop that does not come to a service road. **At about four o'clock, at the sink, she said she was going to have to stop: one of her four employers draws about nine people a week from an agency, that depot is on the same agency, a woman with a clipboard asked her in a corridor about three weeks ago whether she was friendly with anybody on strike, she said no and it was true, and there is nothing in writing anywhere, which is the whole difficulty and the whole of why it works.** Her count and not her claim: nineteen, nine Fridays a week across four employers, none of whom knows of the other three, about ninety pounds, and about two hundred in the account. **A man of twenty-two stood at the back for six hours, said one thing at about half past four that was not wrong and was not enough, and was told that the room's silence about a stranger is the right answer and is not a kindness, and was asked to say what it is, and said that nobody in that room can be asked whether he is right, and was told that being the one who has to be the friend is a job and that she is the only person there who cannot afford it.**

### Chapter 0225 — "Our Method"

**The Thursday evening of week seventy-eight, from about half past seven to about ten, in four rooms off a service road in the Lower Wards, and at about ten at a bench in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 228, and its own opening label says this entry is four lines in a company's typeface and the word our in them.**

**THE VOLUME'S MIDPOINT.** A plastic bag with a strap, about forty pages stapled at the corner, page two carrying the photograph, and beneath it four lines in a company typeface at about nine point: a working method, in a real room; the room asks a person what they do and records it in their own words; nothing is claimed, nothing is standardised, and the practitioner can withdraw at any time. **The printer of fifty-eight read them aloud twice from the counter, unasked, and then finished a sentence the man of twenty-two had started and could not finish, which is the finding of the chapter: ten lines on a hardboard in six hands, the first a cook's about withdrawal and the second a first-year's about claims of improvement, printed in a company's voice with a company's pronoun on page two of a pack with about nine hundred impressions of a leaflet off one counter in one morning.** The clerk of forty-four said the other half: four people have asked her where she got it, one wanted a discount because she had not come through a form, and she took the pack off an open shelf without leaving a note and chose in about four seconds to be the askable party. **A man of fifty-one with a tool bag, on the record about about nine hundred wrong numbers on about nine hundred doors and unable to change one, said the only thing anybody said about the nine hundred: a plate is a service item inside a building and a leaflet is not, and recovery would need about nine hundred visits to a service road that four buses a day serve — and that he has not been thanked and is not going to be.** **A first-year of nineteen stood up and said she was not taking the leaflet down, and gave two reasons: no mechanism exists, and a public withdrawal would make her the only person in this city anybody could consult about how she did it. She then said out loud that she agreed to a photograph in week seventy-two in about four seconds to a woman she has never met, that six people were in the room, that she asked none of them, and that she does not know whether the six in the picture are the six in that room — and was told that she does not, and that this is worse.** A nurse of twenty-four said the one sentence she came for: a person can agree to a photograph of a room and be in the photograph. **The room decided in about four minutes not to write to the company, because a letter from four signatories in a building with a company's number on the outside is a document about that room, and nothing was sent and nothing was drafted and nothing was minuted.** A woman of about thirty was in the room throughout and is not in the photograph and has never been asked why she comes.

### Chapter 0226 — "Six People And Five Of Them Cannot Be Found"

**The Friday morning and afternoon of week seventy-eight, from twenty to eight to about ten, at a depot, in four rooms off a service road in the Lower Wards, and in a repair shop in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 229, and its own opening label says this entry is a telephone call, a correct decision by a company, and a count of six.**

Eleven on the docket, nine in date, a repeat bin-store timer, a car-park callout, and an emergency lighting pack that passed its test with cells two years out of date and was replaced anyway, which is now the reason that stair will never be examined again. **A call to a company's number on a plate with no name on it at about half past nine, answered by a letting clerk of thirty-nine who gave her sentence and was told it was not about the unit and said hold on; about nine minutes in all, none of it anybody's fault.** A woman gave her name, her company and the reason, and asked who was in the photograph and whether they had agreed to it, and a first-year of nineteen counted for four minutes at the counter and got a cook of twenty-seven, herself, a printer of fifty-eight, a man of twenty-nine on an employer's Friday list, a woman of thirty-one with a key and a diary, and a man of twenty-two. **The six figures in the picture are those six, the count closes, and of the six, five cannot be located by anybody outside that room and the one who can be located is the man with the docket; the seventh person in that room, a woman of about thirty with a bag on her lap, is not in the picture and is the only one of the seven whose face is not in it, and nobody asked her about it and about four people wanted to.** The caller announced the withdrawal of the pack from sale, from the next printing and from the course, and the room accepted it and said why it was worth nothing before anybody else could, and the caller said her own part and asked for it to be attributed to her and not to a room, and nobody wrote it down. **A woman of twenty-six who had taken a leaflet off a market table in week seventy and had telephoned the plate on the Tuesday asked whether what people say in that room stays in that room, and got nine words counted on the page — *That photograph is not of a room any more* — and he said out loud before anybody spoke that it is the third such answer and is not an improvement on the two before it.** The clerk of thirty-nine came back, said this is the third time in about two months that the plate has been how somebody found that room, that she holds a sheet of paper with all three calls on it, that it is hers and she is not bringing it here, was asked for it, and refused in one word.

### Chapter 0227 — "Eleven Times In A Corridor, Badly"

**The Monday morning and afternoon of week seventy-nine, from about ten past eight in a corridor to about half past seven in four rooms, and at about twenty past four in a canteen and at about half past seven in the room. Load-book entry 230, and its own opening label says this entry is a piece of hardboard, a corridor, eleven repetitions, two errors that were not corrected, and ninety pounds.**

**A first-year of nineteen bought a piece of hardboard about the size of a tea tray off a man in a builder's merchant for about two pounds, carried it in on foot, and wrote one line on it in her own hand: the first line of that room's wall, and not the other nine, and gave the reason standing up — the other nine are about what a room will not do and this one is about what it will not keep, and a person about to hand something over at a desk needs the second kind.** She stood in a ground-floor clinic corridor in Lattice Ward, about four feet wide and about ninety feet long, with a desk at one end and about nine handovers an hour, eleven feet from the desk so that people going past had to turn their heads. **Nine of about eleven people stopped in about an hour and a half and the line was spoken eleven times.
