It went wrong twice and she corrected neither: once by saying it too fast, and once when a man of about sixty returned it with the sense inverted, and she refused to correct him because correcting a stranger creates a person holding a sentence in the wrong order with no means of finding out, and no form anywhere takes a sentence off a person.** **At about eleven o'clock a woman wrote to the clinic about a girl shouting in a corridor, the letter went on a board by the desk where that clinic displays anything anybody has written, a woman behind the desk read it out to about four people in a queue, nobody rang the first-year about it, and one of her four employers — a Friday at that clinic where she has done the stores for about a year — was covered by somebody else at about eleven that morning. About ninety pounds out of an account of about two hundred, given in a canteen to about nine people who do not know her name.** A man of twenty-two said the thing would be on a wall within a month and photographed, and was told he was about eleven miles away on a docket; he then said she should have asked somebody first, and was told that there is nobody to ask and that this is the entire content of the wall the board was bought for. **And she named the sentence she did not invent: a nurse of twenty-four, whom she has never spoken to, said in a corridor outside a clinic a few months back, to a queue of about nine people, that a clinic asks a person for nine things at a desk before it asks one question about any of it — and that both sentences are true, and that the nurse's is the one that would get a person arrested and hers is the one a person can act on at a desk, and that ninety minutes of saying the actionable one is not nothing and is not very much.** The letter is about a girl shouting in a corridor and it is not wrong and she put her there.

### Chapter 0228 — "A Pair Of Shoes"

**The Tuesday afternoon of week seventy-nine, from about half past two to about half past six, in four rooms off a service road in the Lower Wards, and at about ten at a bench in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 231, and its own opening label says this entry is a pair of shoes and eleven weeks.**

**A man of fifty-eight with the same bad hip came back nineteen days after he was refused, said he had not brought the envelope, and put a pair of shoes on the counter: resoled eleven times on the left and not on the right, cut by hand because a machine does not do that shape, wearable for about an hour and a half, after which the wearer is a person with a stick in a corridor.** He said it was not a practice, not a method, not his sister's and not his; that a man who cuts soles by hand had asked him a question about the heel and the answer had been his own and was on the back of a receipt; and that he would like to leave them where somebody could see them, the reason being that he wanted to be able to pick them up and put them down again without asking anybody. **Told that a pair of shoes is a pair of shoes, that nobody is going to ask him a single question about them, and that they can stay until the shutter comes down and then he takes them home — which is neither a favour nor a rule and is the only thing in that building that has ever worked.** Then the wall, line by line: a thing liftable in about nine seconds satisfies the first line and cannot satisfy a binder or a page, and one page in that binder has been struck through once and cannot be restored; a shoe admits of no copy; a shoe has never been printed; the tenth line was not invoked because no request under it was necessary. **A cook of twenty-seven said the other half and refused to say it twice: a kettle, a sheet of cloth and a pair of shoes are none of them a technique, and a record of any of them would in about four years put a card in front of somebody saying they had failed, and in this case the card would be about a man's feet — but an object may stay on a counter where it is visible and be taken away at the end of a day, and that is a different class of event from a page.** He read his sister's page for about four minutes, found a number in it he has asked her for about two hundred times in nine years and has never heard, established on the room that it exists because a woman telephoned a company's number and was written down by somebody who has never met her, and said she was wrong. **At the door he said four words to a man of twenty-two who had said nothing all afternoon: that this was the first time in eleven weeks that somebody had brought him something he could hand back in a minute. He was told the shoes were handable because of what he had done and not because of the room, and that this was not going to be improved into something about the room.**

### Chapter 0229 — "Who Held The Camera, And Why The Hand Is Moving"

**The Wednesday afternoon of week seventy-nine, from about two to about half past six, in four feet of shop in the Quiet Quarter. Load-book entry 232, and its own opening label says this entry is a shutter speed, three photographs, and a hundred and forty-one pounds that has not moved.**

A counter, a press, about four hundred leaflets left out of nine hundred, a card of the shop's own with a quarterly return on it, and a till with an envelope under it that has no name on it, cannot be sent back, has not moved since the spring, and is a hundred and forty-one pounds. **A person of forty-one with a first floor in Nine Bridges and a van, who has been a customer of that counter for about four years, said they had known since the Wednesday and did not pretend otherwise, and then said the thing nobody in that shop had understood: look at the hand. A binder standing open on a counter with a sheet proud of the sleeve reads in a still exposure as a certificate; at about a fortieth of a second the hand on it moved, and the movement is what reads as a room, and it was set deliberately in about nine seconds for that reason — so that the legibility of six people's handwriting on a printed page is the consequence of a craft decision taken to stop the picture looking like a document. Their summary of the mechanism, said once, without heat, is that no conspiracy and no fault is involved and that the whole of the accident is a shutter speed.** Four exposures in about eleven minutes; a brief that said *a working method, in a real room*, *with people in it if possible*, and *not posed*; one paid for and three not. **A person of thirty-four who buys training for a company, who wrote the brief, said she has never once asked to see a person in a photograph, gave the reason — the room is somebody else's, asking who is in it would require them to identify their own occupants, they would not, and the exchange would have run about nine weeks and she had a course to write — and then said the part she could not improve: a young woman answered a telephone in about four seconds and said yes, she wrote that down in her own book as a yes, and three colleagues that week used the same word about a supplier.** **The printer of fifty-eight said she has been wrong about printed matter four times in forty years and right once, and the once is a ring binder with her own handwriting nine inches tall on the top of page two, and that she is not a mechanism and has never asked to be on that wall, and that the name a reader arrives at is a shop with a telephone number on the outside of it, and that no number on the pack is hers.** And the person of forty-one said they will not destroy the three uncommissioned photographs in a drawer in Nine Bridges, because a person paid for a job and the job was done, and because in about four years a drawer with three pictures of a room and six people in it and no name on the front would be the only evidence in this city that anybody stood in that room who is not in the pack — and that there is no mechanism anywhere for handing three photographs back.

### Chapter 0230 — "Twelve Lines, Seven Things, And A Corridor"

**The Thursday evening of week seventy-nine, from about half past seven to about ten, in four rooms off a service road in the Lower Wards, and at about ten in a repair shop in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 233, and its own opening label says this entry is a wall of twelve lines in seven hands, a binder of seven things, and a corridor.**

About eleven people and four of them standing in about two feet of floor, and a first-year of nineteen stating, for the third time she has said it, that the room is busier and smaller at once and that the man has not been present for one of the two conditions. **A man of twenty-two stood in that floor rather than at the back, for the first time in eleven weeks, unremarked and observed by about nine people.** The wall went from ten lines in six hands and seven names to **twelve lines in seven hands and eight names**. **The eleventh line was written first, by the man of fifty-eight, standing up, and read back to the room before he let anybody know he wanted it there: that it will not keep a thing whose words are somebody else's — eleven words, the only thing he has learned in nineteen days, and he did not have it on the afternoon the room refused his sister's sequence, and he said that is the whole of what the room did. Asha Reed said out loud, in front of about eleven people, that he is the second man of fifty-eight to write in that room and not the first, that the first was the Monday of week seventy and refused to write anything, and that she thinks about him about twice a week.** The twelfth line was hers: that it will tell you what it will not hold before you hand it over. **She said that the room had begun, four days earlier, to do this in a corridor in about ninety minutes with a board bought for about two pounds, said eleven times, had it go wrong twice, once in her mouth and once in a man's, and fixed neither, lost nine Fridays and about ninety pounds, and would do it again; that doing it again is worse than being told; and that both versions of it are on the table.** A man of twenty-nine who works on lifts asked what the corridor is for, and was told it is for the people who are not here, and that it cannot be done every day, cannot be done on a Friday because of four employers of which one is at a clinic, and cannot be done indoors because nine people already know the line. **A nurse of twenty-four gave the finding once: that saying it badly is the only shape available, because doing it well would produce a question about how, and there is no how, and the how is a person, and the person is findable; and that she has never been in a photograph, that this is not modesty and not a rule, and that it is the only arrangement in that building she has been able to rely on.** The binder came off the shelf and was opened on the counter, not on the floor, and has **seven things**; the first-year read the number and the hands and the contents of none of them, as she has done once before, and said that **six of the seven carry the name of a man of twenty-two, that he is the only one of the seven anybody in this city can go and find, and that he and she are both going to do nothing about it**, and that a man of fifty-one with a tool bag has said so to her privately. **A woman of thirty-one with a key and a diary found the sounder loose at the top where a trolley had struck it, put two screws into it in about four minutes, tested it from the service road, and it rang, and nothing happened, because a sound at a man on a step does not make anybody come.** And a woman of about thirty was in the third chair for the whole evening and is not in the photograph and has never been asked why she comes, and about four people in that room wanted to and did not.

---

# VOLUME 05 — MOVEMENT II (Chapters 211–220), "In Their Own Words" — COMPLETE

The load-book run is 214 to 223, continuous, one entry per chapter, no duplicate and no gap, and it does not end here. The ten days are fixed in `workspace/volume-05/batch-0002/PROMPT.md`: five in week seventy-one, three in week seventy-two and two in week seventy-three, and the whole of weeks seventy-four and seventy-five is unmapped. The eight-word overlap of each entry with its own chapter, measured on the files as they stand with the matcher printed in `workspace/volume-04/batch-0005/SUMMARY.md`, is 78, 80, 32, 53, 82, 32, 42, 94, 78 and 105 — a mean of 67.6 and a range of 32 to 105. The batch is 31,905 words (18,455 prose, 13,450 load book), 560 raw instances of *about* at 17.55 per thousand, **401 true hedges at 12.57 on the one reading the printed rule produces — the article in the rule is optional, so the strict and chain branches are a single matcher and this batch's first draft of this paragraph printed 387 and 401 as two readings and there is only one** — and 238 bold spans at 7.46, worst chapter 9.54, and the two ceilings in the prompt are bold ceilings and the bold figure is inside both. The hedge matcher reproduces four of the six earlier published hedge figures to the unit and does not reproduce two of them, and neither of the two is explained. A repair pass ran after the batch was written and found thirty-one defects, of which twenty-two were date intervals, twenty were figures in documents written to be read by somebody who was not going to check them, twenty-eight were bold spans in the narration, eleven lines had odd marker counts and six load-book lines had a nested span. **A review repair pass then ran over the batch and its documents and found ten more: two of them were in the chapters — Chapter 215's prose said the market was ten days back where its own entry said nine, and Chapter 220's bench paragraph opened with the same ninety-four characters as Chapter 215's — and eight of them were unreproducible figures in the summary and the state files, including a headline hedge figure, a matcher-provenance claim that said one published figure in six reproduces when four do, a backward sweep that had walked a remembered subset and reported twenty runs where there are a hundred and five, a within-batch pair count of seventy-five where ten chapters have forty-five pairs, and a false claim of nil duplicated paragraphs. Every one is fixed. No chapter was restarted, no scene was rewritten, no planned event moved, and the two chapter edits are one figure in a sentence and one reworded paragraph. The log is in `workspace/volume-05/batch-0002/SUMMARY.md`.**

### Chapter 0211 — "Two Keys And A Reason"

**The Monday morning of week seventy-one, from about half past nine to about half past one, in four rooms off a service road in the Lower Wards, and at ten that night in a repair shop in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 214, and its own opening label says this entry is a second key, a sheet of typing paper, and a question four people could answer and one they could not.**

A woman of thirty-one with a key and a diary put a cut key with a faded paper label on a counter with a sheet of typing paper on which one reason is written in her own hand: you can ring her, and she will come and take it out of your hand inside a day. **A first-year of nineteen turned the reason into one question on the back of a docket — who can be asked to take this key back — and four people answered it in about nine seconds, three of them about a person, and the answer about the first key is nobody.** The finding, in his order: a person holding a key is not a post until in about four years a person with a key is a post, and the whole difference is whether the people who handed it over can take it back, and the people who handed over the first one are a company with a ring file, and **a key nobody can take back is a key whose holder can never be relieved, so the first key is not the one that gives the room a door, it is the one that makes him a holder, and it made him a holder the day it came off that counter.** Nobody invented a holder, no lock was fitted, no list was written, and the first key did not go back in an envelope. The first-year said the hard version first. The giver named her cost unasked: her name on a sheet of typing paper in a room with a bell-push on it, after four years of being careful, and she is going to be findable about one thing. Nobody thanked her. **A woman of about thirty was in the room for the fifth time, and a first-year said the reason out loud, and the woman gave it back and asked to be left in the chair and not be the thing a person is careful about.** Nobody was careful about her.

### Chapter 0212 — "Twenty-Two Minutes With Four People In It"

**The Tuesday morning of week seventy-one, from about ten to about half past eleven, in four rooms off a service road in the Lower Wards, and at ten that night on a counter in the front room with the shutter half down. Load-book entry 215, and its own opening label says this entry is four people, twenty-two minutes, and four hands on one page.**

Four people, about twenty-two minutes, five beats, four roles, and the fifth beat is that it is not finished until the kettle goes on. A cook of twenty-seven on her own time from a kitchen that does not open until the afternoon, a man of twenty-nine who works on lifts who came on a Tuesday for the first time, a woman of thirty-one with a key and a diary, and a printer of fifty-eight who had been in that room twice and had said about nine words both times. **At about eighteen minutes the printer began a sentence about where something went and three other people finished it, and the arrangement is as old as the room, and four people in a room heard their own arrangement nine seconds after he did and he said nothing.** He stood at the back with a docket for twenty-two minutes, did not take part, was not asked and did not offer. **The reason he did not comment was said out loud by the cook with her hands in a sink and not by him: it is not modesty, she has worked behind a man at the front of a kitchen for nine years and knows what it costs, and a room that waits for the man at the front is a room with a man at the front of it. He agreed in about four seconds, said he was not going to improve on it, and was sent out to stand on a service road for ten minutes.** A first-year of nineteen timed it on her wrist, wrote the number on the back of her hand in biro and had to rub it out with a thumb for about a minute, and has said since that the rubbing out was the most useful thing anybody did that morning. **The four of them wrote one page in four hands in about half an hour, and she held it flat and wrote none of it and put the pen down beside it rather than in her hand, because if she writes any of it then it is her words about their thing.** She then argued for about four minutes that the page ought to say the room was not empty, was told the page she was holding flat is the line that says the room was not empty, and gave the reason: a page which says who was standing in a room is a page which describes a room.

### Chapter 0213 — "Nine Chairs And A Cup Of Tea"

**The Wednesday afternoon and evening of week seventy-one, from about twenty past one to about ten, in four rooms off a service road in the Lower Wards, and at ten that night in a repair shop in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 216, and its own opening label says this entry is nine chairs, a sheet, four people doing it badly on purpose, and a kettle.**

A woman of thirty-eight who has folded sheets for nineteen years in four places about eleven miles apart offered at about twenty past one to show anybody who walked in how she folds a sheet corner to corner in three, flat, in about eleven seconds, with about four minutes and a bus after it, and got the bus. **About eleven people came through the door in an hour and nine were in the room at once, which is the number of chairs and was an accident, and four of the nine had been asked before they came to do it badly on purpose, and nobody in the room knew which four, and the woman offering the thing never knew and never would have.** The reason, given out loud in about nine words, is the only sentence in this movement nobody argued with: a demonstration is a demonstration because somebody in it can tell whether you did it right, and the test is not the fold. One offer, four demonstrations, nine attempts, zero correct, about four results that were not cloth any more, one triangle from a young man who had come in to be warm. **Nothing was corrected for half an hour, and the two people in the room who could have corrected something did not, and a cook of twenty-seven named the cost afterwards at a sink: about four wrong folds in the first four minutes, one of which was going to end up as a sheet somebody's child had slept under, nine minutes of silence, the longest nine minutes she has spent in that room, and she is not going to be told it was the right thing again.** A man of twenty-two was by the back wall for the whole half hour with a docket, was asked nothing, did not go round the room and said nothing about any of the nine. **The thing that worked was the kettle, and about eight of nine people sat down, and two mugs were short, and a first-year of nineteen asked to record it and was refused in front of about nine people: a technique in that room is a thing whose wrongness hurts a person, and if the kettle is recorded then in about four years there is a person somewhere not doing it right, and that person made somebody a cup of tea, and there is no card anybody can put in front of her saying she failed.** The binder stayed shut. The four came to the door and told the woman of thirty-eight, and she said she had taught that fold to about four hundred people and somebody was always frightened of getting it wrong in front of her, and the fold does not care, and she did not know that until a man she had never met put the kettle on.

### Chapter 0214 — "The Sixth Person Brings Something"

**The Thursday afternoon of week seventy-one, from about two to about four, in four rooms off a service road in the Lower Wards, and at ten that night in a repair shop in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 217, and its own opening label says this entry is a page that went in and came out in ninety seconds, and two lines of a wall.**

A man of thirty-three on a heating company's rota, in twice before and bringing nothing, brought the first nine minutes of a job: stand in the hall about four seconds, look at the ceiling, look at the outside of the nearest radiator, read the labels on the consumer unit without touching anything, and only then go and find what you came for. **A first-year of nineteen wrote it down in his own words in about four minutes, kept the two words he had replaced, did not tidy the sentence about the four seconds, and the page went into the ring binder as its fourth thing, and he read it back twice and said it was what he said.** Ninety seconds later he said that the nine minutes are printed on his employer's job sheet with his name in the box at the bottom since he was twenty-four, on about eleven thousand pieces of paper in about nine hundred vans, and that the second they are on a page in a binder they are that sheet. **The larger half of his reason is his own: he has been in that room twice and could not tell anybody who is in it, and a page in a room he cannot describe, in a building with a company's telephone number on the outside of it, is a page he cannot take back, and if his company finds out the first thing that goes is that number and the second is a year four people have signed.** A first-year read two lines off the wall — that it will not keep a copy of a thing nobody asked to keep, and that it will not be printed — and he agreed in about four seconds, took the page out of the binder himself, one handed, folded it once and put it in his inside pocket. He named his cost unasked: he is the only person in this city who knows the four words by which his version differs from the printed one, and at ten that morning he was the only one and now four people are. **The first-year said out loud that this is her own argument, made on a floor three weeks ago and on a counter the week before, that she lost it twice, and that she is not going to act as though a man of thirty-three taught her something — and he agreed, said that is why he mentioned the telephone number on the outside of the building before his own name at the bottom of a sheet, and she had no answer.**

### Chapter 0215 — "Eleven Jobs And A Woman On Foot"

**The Friday of week seventy-one, from twenty to eight in the morning to about ten at night, at a depot, in two stairwells in Nine Bridges, in a cold store, in a canteen off a service road, in four rooms off a service road in the Lower Wards, and at ten at night in a repair shop in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 218, and its own opening label says this entry is eleven jobs, a stair fitting nobody asked him to change, a man with nothing, and a woman on foot. THIS IS THE FIRST FRIDAY ROTA OF MOVEMENT II.**

Eleven jobs, nine in date, grade three from twenty to eight, thirty-four degrees in a stairwell, nine men in a yard at twenty to eight. A stair light, two lift calls, three landing lights of which two were not faults, a fitting on a stair off a service road, a cold-store door, a tap. **A cold-store door that closes, two readings with his own instrument, both correct, logged, and a man of about forty-four in a white coat who said the figure had held for a month and that the other three visitors in a month were also right. A lamp inside tolerance that he replaced with an identical lamp, which made no difference. And a fitting of a type not made since before his apprenticeship, of which about nine hundred stand in four buildings and of which about nine hundred of the current type have already gone in over about two years, changed although the job sheet asked for a lamp and although nobody asked him to, because the old one is what will be a problem in four years, and it made no difference that day and it will be the reason nobody ever examines that stair.** The word went from nine a minute to ten at about one in the afternoon and was still ten at ten at night. Nobody asked him where a release was going to come from, which has been true on every Friday of this case for a term and a half. **A man of twenty-nine who works on lifts came in at about seven on his own time on the day his employer's list runs on, had nothing, wanted nothing, was asked nothing, sat down at about eight and left at about half past nine, and said at the door that this is the best hour and a half he has had in four years, and did not explain it and has not been asked.** And a woman of about sixty-eight walked from a bus stop because the bus does not come to a service road, having read a leaflet in about eleven hundred pockets, and asked whether she could be told what happens in there, and got nine words counted on the page: *I could not tell you what happens in there.* **He said out loud, before anybody else spoke, that it is not an improvement on the nine words he said in a market nine days before, and gave the difference: the first set was nine words about the room and this set is nine words about himself.** She said she had come to the wrong place, and was told that she had come to a room with nine chairs and about four people in it who had not asked her a question. She stayed about forty minutes, asked for nothing, drank from the one unchipped mug in the building, and said at the door that they have got a good room and that what happens in a good room is nothing. **The page stayed empty for the second time in a month and for the second different reason: the first time a category would have been created, and this time there is no column in this city for a person being right.**

### Chapter 0216 — "The First Line, Read Aloud"

**The Monday morning of week seventy-two, from about ten to about half past twelve, in four rooms off a service road in the Lower Wards, and at ten that night in a repair shop in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 219, and its own opening label says this entry is a laminated sheet that stayed on a counter, and the first line of a wall.**

A man of forty-nine, nine years in a canteen in a building off a service road with about eleven hundred people in it, offered a thing he has taught about a hundred and forty people: carrying four hot plates at once, two hands each, the second two starting before the first two land, a rhythm and not a trick. He put a laminated sheet on the counter and said before anybody touched it that it is not his. **The structural reason, given unasked: if the room holds it in his own words then there are two versions, and the one in a binder with a person's name at the top of it is the one that will read as the correction, and nobody has to say that out loud for a correction to exist, and in about four years somebody finds out their sheet is not the only one and changes it, and nine canteens have a superseded thing on a wall and about a hundred and forty people were taught by a man who is not the man on the new sheet.** A first-year of nineteen put a hand flat on the binder on the shelf and did not open it, went and read the first line off the hardboard, and said the man had worked it out at the table before she reached the wall, which is why she said it anyway. The sheet went back into its pocket and the binder stayed on the shelf with three things in it. **At the door he said the finding of the movement: the nine lines have no force, nobody in this city could make him obey them, he would have been within the law for about four years, and he did not argue with a wall because in the previous week a man took somebody else's page out of that binder and he does not know the man's name and does not need to.** She answered in about nine words: that is not a rule that works, that is four people who happened to be in a room, it has worked twice, and one of the times it cost the person it was done to. He said: then it has worked twice. A cook of twenty-seven said that one of the nine lines stopped a man that morning and it is the only thing in that building that has ever stopped anybody. A woman of about thirty was in the third chair for about two hours and was asked nothing. A man of twenty-two was at the back with a docket for the whole of it and was asked nothing and said nothing.

### Chapter 0217 — "Forty-Five"

**The Wednesday morning of week seventy-two, from about half past ten to about eleven, in a back room with the shutters up, and at ten that night in a repair shop in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 220, and its own opening label says this entry is a fourth-column book that opened after six sittings shut, and a price that was said out loud. THIS IS THE EXCHANGE AND THE BOOK GOES TO FORTY-FIVE HERE AND NOWHERE ELSE IN THIS VOLUME.**

Seven sittings on the Wednesdays of weeks 48, 52, 56, 60, 64, 68 and 72, six gaps of four weeks, six of the seven at which the book did not open now counted as six and a half, and a woman of sixty-one said out loud that nobody in this city can say what a half is meant to mean. **The telephone count moved from twenty-nine to thirty-one to thirty-three and was said out loud because it is the only thing in that room that has moved all year; she said she has said it out loud there every four weeks and twelve times, and reconciled the twelve against the book's seven herself and unasked — seven of those are in this book, the first five are not and are not going to be — so the twelve is a figure about her and not about the sittings in the book, and the room existed before the book did. Two people rang in the four weeks since, one of them is in the room and one is not, and she is not going to say which of the four callers in eight weeks is the one on the morning and gave the same reason as in the spring and refused to give it a third time.** Two items at four minutes each, neither recorded. **The tin, which has no lock on it and is not a system, was opened for about eleven seconds for the first time in nineteen years, and only after the caller declined to say her words standing up, on the ground that a word is not taken off anybody, a word nobody meant is not taken either, and a word said in a car goes into that book as *she said it in a car*, which is the worst line in it.** She had telephoned in the spring, had been told to come on a Wednesday, had said she would think about it, has thought about it for about four months, said before sitting down that she nearly did not come, and said that she telephoned because somebody wrote down something she said, that it was not a doctor, that it was a room with a form in it, that she was not asked anything, and that she will not say what she said. **Line forty-five is her own words in the first column, nothing in the second, her own name in the fourth, and the book was turned round and pushed four inches across the table and read back to her before she was allowed to sign it with a pen off that table.** The price was named out loud by the woman of sixty-one and nobody asked her to and she did not improve the rule and did not shorten it and did not thank anybody: for nineteen years the answer to why the book had forty-four lines and not forty-five was a rule, and now it is that the book grew once, and an answer of that shape means a person is attached to it, and anybody in this city may now telephone and ask when it is going to grow again, which is the only question she has ever been asked that she has no answer to. She did not thank the caller and she did not thank a man of twenty-two who has been in nine of these sittings and said nothing for about two hours, and was told she could not stop saying it and he could not either. Nobody wrote anything down. A first-year of nineteen heard the book open and has not said one word about it, and the woman of sixty-one has still never once used her name in that room.

### Chapter 0218 — "A Person In A Room And A Person In A Sentence"

**The Thursday evening of week seventy-two, from about seven to about half past eight, in four rooms off a service road in the Lower Wards, and at ten that night in a repair shop in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 221, and its own opening label says this entry is a page that was right and wrong in the same four minutes, and a rule that was not written.**

A man of thirty-one who fits hearing aids, nine years, about four hundred people a week, had been in the room twice the week before and folded a sheet badly on purpose both times without knowing it. **What he does is listen to a room for about three seconds without asking anybody anything, and the three signs of a person who has stopped wearing an aid are that they go on answering on time, that they turn further than they need to, and that they look at a mouth about nine inches below the mouth, and none of the three is a fault in the aid.** A first-year of nineteen asked what it is for, and he answered at her request for about a minute and a half in front of about nine people, and the answer is a claim about a use: it is for when somebody has stopped, because a person who has stopped will go on answering for months and nobody finds out, and the moment of finding out is the moment somebody tells them they have been talking to a wall for a year. **Nobody in that room can say the question was wrong and everybody can say it was the right one, and the chapter turns on that.** She wrote a page in his own words in about four minutes, not tidied, and it reads as a set of three things to look at, which are the three things he has been doing since he was twenty-two and have never once been the three seconds. **He read it back twice and said it was not what he had said, and said immediately that it was in his words and in the right order and could not have been written better in about eleven minutes by anybody, and that nobody had done anything wrong.** He had given the use and not the practice, and the use is the easy one and nobody can do the easy one badly, and the practice cannot be printed, cannot be put on a wall, and cannot be learned off a person in four minutes. **A nurse of twenty-four at the back of the room said the finding once: about nine times a year she reads back what a person said to her and it is not what they said, and it has never been because they lied, and a person in a room is doing a thing and a person in a sentence has got a reason attached, and you cannot get the reason off and keep the thing.** The first-year named the fifth thing in that binder herself, out loud: a use. She said her two years of carefulness made a false page. **She then asked for a rule fixing which question gets asked and was told no before she had finished, on the ground that a rule about which question gets asked is a rule about which people get asked, and that it costs about nine minutes and the nine minutes are the price of the book being worth having.** The page went into his own inside pocket and the binder went back on the shelf with three things in it.

### Chapter 0219 — "One Stroke"

**The Monday morning of week seventy-three, from about eleven to about half past twelve, in four rooms off a service road in the Lower Wards, and at ten that night in a repair shop in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 222, and its own opening label says this entry is one pen stroke, about nine people, a box in pencil in another building, a line added to a wall, and the cost. THIS IS THE REMOVAL AND IT IS NOT A SCENE.**

At about eleven, at a sink, before the kettle, with about nine people in the room and the shutter up, **the first person ever recorded in that room asked to be taken out of it.** The item is the eleventh of eleven on the first sheet of the binder: a person's own name, spelled as its owner spells it, entered in a second hand at a cook of twenty-seven's request because she cannot spell it. **Its owner telephoned her at work on the Thursday and asked for it to be taken off, and the cook reported that in the room in about nine words and said the reason is about a piece of paper and not about her: she is the person who put it there, she entered it on a judgement that a name is a courtesy, the other ten items on that sheet are hers, and the eleventh was not hers to give, and a name on a page in a binder on a shelf in a room with a bell-push on it is a name anybody can look up, and no form in this city takes a name off a page.** Three obstacles were raised out loud: the name is not in the cook's hand and its owner is four miles away and would not come; writing the question out first is a page and this room does not have those; **and a first-year of nineteen said plainly that she was not choosing him — there is exactly one person in that room whom anybody in this city can telephone, and he said it himself on that floor the Thursday he signed the hardboard.** He asked for the question to be put aloud so that he would not be the one deciding it, said he cannot answer it as a rule because a rule is not enforceable against the sister who is not in the room, and said he is going to do it because there is nobody else. **A biro out of his own pocket; the first sheet; he did not read it, did not touch the other ten items and did not go round the page; one stroke, top left to bottom right, in about a second and a half, not retraced and not tidied. A woman of about thirty was in the third chair from the door and watched it and was asked nothing. The room was silent for about eleven seconds and the cook said one word. Nobody stopped it and nobody was asked to.** It cannot be undone, because a finger takes the ink off and leaves the paper, and if the stroke is rubbed out at about four months then at about four years somebody opens that binder and finds a name with a ghost through it and nothing to show that anybody asked, and the only trace of the request would be a smudge. The first-year said she would argue with the fact that he is the only one who can be asked rather than with him. **The cost, in two voices: he named a box ruled in pencil in a building in Bower Street with a name in it that cannot be extracted, corrected or joined by a second, and said he has been the reason it is the only one of its kind for about a term and a half; she priced the whole operation at four minutes, a five-mile walk each way, a sandwich and half a morning of somebody's Saturday, called it the cheapest thing anybody in this case has ever bought, and said immediately that she does not think she is allowed to say that as a happy thing, and said it.** **The wall gained a tenth line in the cook's own hand: it will take it back if you ask. A man of twenty-two said the room cannot have a procedure because a procedure is a rule and a rule is unenforceable against somebody who is not in the room, and then said something he had said in Chapter 206 in different words without knowing it: a wall is not a set of rules, it is a set of things people wrote down because they needed them written down, and the second of the nine is the truest thing in the room.** The first-year said a person being wrong about what they said is a fifth thing in that binder. The stroke was not read aloud, not explained and not minuted. A nurse of twenty-four said the cook's surname from the back of the room once and did not come back for about an hour.

### Chapter 0220 — "Six Things In A Binder"

**The Thursday evening of week seventy-three, from about half past seven to about half past ten, in four rooms off a service road in the Lower Wards, and at ten that night in a repair shop in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 223, and its own opening label says this entry is six things in a binder, five of them in five hands, one page with a name struck through it in a single stroke and not erased, and a woman of about thirty who is the only person in that room not on anything.**

About eleven people in a room with nine chairs and four of them standing in about two feet of floor, and a first-year of nineteen did the arithmetic out loud and got it right: busier than it was, smaller than it was, both at once, and a person standing in a room is a person who is not staying. **The binder came off the shelf and was opened flat on a mat on the floor and has six things in it — eleven items in a cook's hand with the eleventh struck through in a single stroke, four words in a first-year's hand, the four hands of the first composite thing, and one page each from a printer of fifty-eight, a man of twenty-nine who works on lifts and a woman of thirty-one with a key and a diary. Five of the six are in five hands and one has four. The last three were written on days that are not in any chapter of this movement, in their own hands, by nobody who was asked, and no file has a date for any of them.** She read the six out loud and read the contents of none of them and said anybody who wants to know what is on the struck page can go and look at it, and nobody asked. **Five of the six carry the answer to a question one man asked, because the room's whole method is a question and the only person in this city who can be asked to answer for it is him, and he said so on that floor a fortnight and a half before this. The sixth has four hands on it and not one of them is his: he was in that room for twenty-two minutes with a docket, was not asked to take part, did not offer, was given the reason by a cook of twenty-seven, agreed in about four seconds, and would not have permitted himself to be put on the page, and nobody did.** So he is on five of the six and is the only one of the people whose hands those five are in that anybody in this city can go and find. **And a woman of about thirty — not a practitioner, came in because it was raining, asked for nothing since the Friday of week sixty-nine, not on the wall, not in the binder, not on the scrap, not in any file — said the finding once, to him and not to the room: a man with one of nine keys is a man with a door and the whole defence is that nobody can produce the list, and you have put the same name on five of six pages and nobody does that by accident, and a man on five of six pages is a document, and there is no version of this that is not a trade, because if yours is not on them then somebody else's has to be and a cook and a printer of fifty-eight have both said their reasons for not giving theirs.** He did not answer it, it was not written down, it is not on the wall, and nobody asked her why she was there. A nurse of twenty-four on her knees with a mat said that that is the finding. A printer of fifty-eight was not there and sent nothing. A man of twenty-nine sat in the fourth chair at about nine and was asked nothing for about an hour and a half. **About eleven people a week in this city are still going to be taught that a release waits for a man in a lanyard, about four of them are not on a rota, the figure is on the back of a woman's own card and is on no form, and a name on five of six pages is not a rota.** And at about half past nine a man of about thirty-eight who took four minutes at the Exchange eight days earlier and wanted to know what happens if he does not sign something at work signed it on the Thursday and has not slept, and came to the door to say that he had spent eight days deciding whether to ask the only person in that room anybody could ask, that he got to the door with the man about nine feet away, and that he did not come over, **and that this is the first useful thing anybody has done for him in about a month**; and he gave for nothing that the woman at work said that is all right we all sign them, that he has been repeating it for five nights, and that the thing he cannot work out is that a room four miles away has a wall on it with a particular line on it and about four people know the cost of it. **He could not answer that and said goodnight, and a cook of twenty-seven told him the binder is not his, that he is the only one of the six the telephone rings for, and that if that is a fault the fault is about this city and not about him, and that she had said all of it once and was going to bed.**
