### Chapter 0201 — "A Bell That Does Not Ring"

**The Monday morning of week sixty-eight, from about half past nine to about eleven, in four rooms off a service road in the Lower Wards, and at ten that night at a bench in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 204, and its own opening label says this entry is four rooms off a service road, nine chairs, a counter, a ring binder on a shelf, two plug sockets of which one works, a bell-push that is not connected to anything, a window onto a wall about four feet away, a floor that is not level, and a door that wants about a millimetre off the top edge and nothing else.**

A converted shop unit: a shutter, a counter, nine chairs counted twice on one morning by two people who did not know the other was counting, a ring binder with nothing in it, two plug sockets of which one works, a bell-push four inches left of the door that has never been connected to anything, a window onto a wall about four feet away, a floor that is not level, a back door that sticks a foot into the room. **A letting clerk of thirty-nine put her ring file on the counter, did not open it, and stopped his pen.** Her reason, unasked: four people writing down what they decided in a room with a door in it means that in about four years the page is the room, the page can be carried, a page that can be carried can be shown to somebody who was not in the room, and the four of them are then the four people who decided it, in a flat, with no way to say they were young. **He put the pen away in about four seconds and it is the first cost of the room.** He made the true objection in one sentence — if nobody writes it down it stops when the four of them stop coming — and she agreed and gave the other half, a fire in her own office in the spring and about nine boxes of paper and one man's handwriting. The freehold is an entry on a schedule of title and the person who owns it was never met; the first year is at no rent with no second-year figure on the page; the frontage on this side of the row has been empty eleven years and there is a plan in a council file she read and would not describe; **the reason is a sentence about a vacant frontage, given to four sets of people and not improved.** The tea was the first thing anybody made for another person and there is a mug with a chip in the handle.

### Chapter 0202 — "Sixth Wednesday"

**The Wednesday of week sixty-eight, from about half past ten in the morning to about eleven, in a back room with the shutters up, and at ten that night at a bench in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 205, and its own opening label says this entry is a sixth Wednesday, a number that has gone up by two, and nine words said by a person of nineteen sitting on a floor. THIS IS THE EXCHANGE.**

A back room, shutters up, a table, a tin with no lock, a four-column book at forty-four lines, four minutes an item, no vote for anybody, eight chairs and a ninth against the wall with its back to the room. **Six sittings at which the book has not been opened — the Wednesdays of weeks forty-eight, fifty-two, fifty-six, sixty, sixty-four and sixty-eight — six sittings and five gaps of four weeks.** The fortieth line is three years old and has a man's name under it. Line forty-four has a hole in it where a woman of sixty-eight in bay four ought to be. **The telephone count moves from twenty-nine to thirty-one for the first time in a term: two calls in four weeks, neither caller came, she has rung nobody, and the reason is that the day she starts ringing people to ask them to come to a room in there, the room is a woman on a telephone, and a woman on a telephone can be asked where a room is.** Two items, four minutes each, a card out of a catalogue rather than the book. **A first-year of nineteen, sitting on the floor because the eight chairs were taken, says nine words at the man and not at the woman of sixty-one: *You have a room, and you speak for us.*** And then the reason in four parts, the best of which is that nobody in a room has to say anything to be described by the person who has the room. Nine is not a number she would use in that room and she used it anyway. The rule was given in one go and not shortened. **The book did not open, the tin went back into a drawer with no lock on it, and nobody wrote anything down.**

### Chapter 0203 — "Four Lists"

**The Thursday morning of week sixty-eight, from about eleven to about half past one, in the second room of a shop unit, and at ten that night at a bench in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 206, and its own opening label says this entry is four lists on nine sheets of paper, and a leaflet with one sentence on it that has not gone in a market.**

Four people, a ring binder, about nine sheets of paper, two hours and forty minutes, and nothing settled. **A cook of twenty-seven's eleven items, which are what she cooks, the eleventh written by somebody else because it is her sister's name and goes on the wall and not in her mouth. A first-year of nineteen's four: a cure, a promise, a result, an improvement. A nurse of twenty-four's five, which are what the room has to refuse to do, the last of them that it will not keep anything overnight. And his one line, about a person running a practice with a boundary and a fourth step and somebody watching the fourth step happen** — and then the fourth condition, whole and in the order it has to be said in, said once in this case by the man it is on, and the finding: **he cannot do that line, not in that room, not on a Saturday, not with the door shut, and the reason the condition exists is a man of twenty with two cut tendons in a forearm who is alive and has a kitchen.** **The first private friction of the volume is about public testimony and not about a technique.** He wanted the room described in public and read out one sentence with no name on it; she said that a description in public is a description of the people in it, and that he had not got round it by not putting a name on the front. **He did not drop the leaflet, she did not agree to it, and neither of them said one word about anything that happened under a building in Crown Terrace three weeks ago.** No decision, no vote, no form.

### Chapter 0204 — "Eleven Jobs"

**The Friday of week sixty-eight, from about twenty to eight in the morning to about twenty past ten at night, at a depot, on the fourth floor of a block in Nine Bridges, on two landings, and at a bench in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 207, and its own opening label says this entry is a fourth floor in Nine Bridges, a man of thirty-four, and a sentence that had been sitting in him since the spring.**

Eleven jobs, nine in date, grade three from eight. A stair light already off at the isolator by a man in a district jacket who wrote it on his own sheet; a fire door adjusted for the fourth time in six weeks by three contractors who did not know about each other, where the fourth adjustment was right; a ballast done for the fourth time; a tap; two lift calls; three lights on landings. **Nine out of eleven jobs on a Friday being a light or a fire door, and that is a fact about the job sheet and not about anybody.** The fifth job is a shared lighting circuit on the fourth floor in Nine Bridges that drops out at about six every evening; nine working tubes and one not; the one that is not has a third-party fitting on somebody else's correct order from about four months ago. **He wrote *see order, third party* in the box where a job gets described and wrote no name, and did not ask the woman of about twenty-six what the thing was, and that is the correct decision.** She has sixteen Fridays written on the back of her own card and has said the thing to that floor about nine times and says she has got better at saying it and worse at believing it. **At about twenty past ten a man of thirty-four on nights, eleven years on the same rota, stood in his own doorway with his coat on, counted to two, and said the sentence he has been sitting on since the spring: he has told four people for a term and a half that a release waits for a man in a lanyard, has never once said that man's name, and if he were killed on that service road tomorrow the sentence would come out of his mouth on Friday exactly the same.** He wanted about four minutes and had them and was going to sleep. **He is on no page and nothing in this case has a record of it.**

### Chapter 0205 — "Four Names On A Sheet"

**The Monday morning and afternoon of week sixty-nine, from about ten to about half past three, on a counter in a shop unit, on a service road, and at ten that night at a bench in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 208, and its own opening label says this entry is a lease, nine clauses, a void on a weekly list, and four names on one sheet.**

Seven of nine clauses read out in about nine minutes and all seven ordinary and true; then the two she has not read to anybody, which are the absence of a second-year figure from the page, the schedule and the file, and a ninth that says if four people sign they are the four people with nothing about what happens if one of them stops. **There is no clause nine(b) and she looked, and every set of people in four years has asked her about clause eight and not one of them about clause nine.** The sentence about a vacant frontage is given four times and not improved; on the fifth asking she gives a different one — the unit is a void, a void has been on a weekly list since the spring before last, a void on a list is visible to somebody above her, and the reason she has never asked is that the last time she did a manager explained for about nine minutes why a question about a void is a problem for whoever wrote it. **Then four people signed, and it is the first document in this case with four names on it, and each named the cost out loud before signing: a cook whose work address and this address would become one address to a licensing man with a clipboard; a nurse who has spent four years making sure nothing anybody can look up has her name at the top of it, and who gave as her own reason that a room with two names on it is a man's room; a first-year for whom it is the first thing with an address on it that she has ever signed and who would rather stop being careful in a room with a bell-push in it than in a common room with a kettle in it; and a man of twenty-two who named the card in the rail, said the reason does not help, and signed.** Nobody thanked the woman with the ring file and she left before anybody could. **On a service road he said four words — *that is a mistake* — and was told the arithmetic is one name and not four, that she has just signed, that he has just watched her sign, and he has no answer to it.**

### Chapter 0206 — "Bring What You Do"

**The Wednesday morning of week sixty-nine, from about eleven to about half past one, in the front room of a shop unit, and at ten that night at a bench in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 209, and its own opening label says this entry is a board, a thumb, and one key.**

A sign-writer of about sixty, forty-one years, about four thousand of these and about three thousand nine hundred the same six words, offered a plate with a name on it and a telephone number whether you want one or not. **Six letters were written on A4 on the Monday night, torn up, written again on the Tuesday, and read off a counter face up by a first-year of nineteen who did not ask him what it was.** Four words instead, in his hand, on hardboard the size of a tea tray with the letters four inches high, inside by the door at the height of a person's shoulder, not screwed to anything: *Bring what you do.* **A name cannot be taken off aluminium with anything and a word can be taken off a board with a thumb, and that is the entire difference, and he has never in his life had anybody ask him for the second one.** He left a piece of sandpaper on the counter, was paid four pounds and was not thanked. **The argument took half an hour in three positions and the man of twenty-two was not the strongest of them**: a room with a key and a bell-push is the same object as a card in a rail and he can see the larger one from the doorway; a nurse of twenty-four against, in nine words and then in forty, on the ground that a room that cannot be found is a room for people who already know, and who four days earlier had said the reverse and been told the conclusion was wrong, so the two of them now have one each; a cook of twenty-seven against on a kitchen, twice in nine years found by a man with a clipboard, both times with the owner's name on the door; and a first-year of nineteen agreeing in one sentence and then saying one sentence too many about people ringing him. **And one key, handed over on a counter, not a set, and it is the first object he carries in this case that somebody else could ask him about at a door.** A key is not a post, and the reason has not been tested, and a company is not a person and cannot be asked to take anything back.

### Chapter 0207 — "It Was Warm In There"

**The Friday evening of week sixty-nine, from about half past seven to about half past ten, in four rooms off a service road, and at half past ten that night on a counter in the front room with the shutter half up. Load-book entry 210, and its own opening label says this entry is a cup of tea, a bell that rings, and a page that was left empty.**

A woman of about thirty came through a shutter that goes up by hand at seven, unlet in by anybody, not on a rota, not on a course, not referred, with a carrier bag holding two things that stayed on her lap and neither of which ever came out. **She gave her own reason in about eleven seconds: it had been raining since about four, it was warm in there, and she had thought it was a letting agent's office with the lights on. And a second true thing about herself, that a person who has been in a room twenty minutes and been given a cup of tea has not thought anything, and that a person who wants somebody to have thought something is going to be disappointed and she would rather say it in the first minute than the fortieth.** She asked for one thing in the whole visit and got it. **A cook of twenty-seven made it and said the thing she has repeated about four times: nine years is what a cup of tea is, and about four of them a week are somebody doing it alone on a Tuesday with no hot water, and she did not say the word *technique* and nobody in the room said it for her.** A man of twenty-nine on his employer's Friday list took about a millimetre off the top edge of a back-room door with a hand plane, because a man of twenty-two had said on the Monday that it wanted that; nobody asked him, nobody paid him, and no line anywhere carries his name against it. **And a man of about forty-one fitted a second bell-push and a sounder in about forty minutes, and the bell rings, and nothing changes: it was tested from outside only, because from outside is where a person is when they press it, and a sounder makes a noise at a man on a step and does not make anybody come.** Asha Reed did not write the twenty minutes down and gave the reason: the day a woman who came in wet is written down, the binder has a category in it, and she would be having it because somebody turned up on a Friday and not because anybody came to be taught. **The page is left empty. The cook agrees. He writes nothing either, which is the third time in a term and a half.**

### Chapter 0208 — "Somebody On The Other Side"

**The Monday morning and afternoon of week seventy, from about eleven to about four, in a room off a service road, on a service road, and at ten that night in a canteen off a service road with tickets on a spike. Load-book entry 211, and its own opening label says this entry is four steps of which three are portable and one is not, and a schedule with the same number on it twice.**

A man of fifty-eight who inspects buses, thirty-one years on the same round, a flat cap and a bus timetable used as writing paper, who waited until about eleven and did not say he was going to. **His four steps: a door that has dropped into its own seal; do not force it, you will take the hinge off; two hands on the leaf, low, at the hinge side; take the weight of it onto your hip and let it find itself; and then somebody on the far side takes it off the seal and walks it open.** He would not perform it because he would do it wrong, and a man of fifty-eight doing a thing badly in front of a first-year is the one thing nobody can put back afterwards. When the fourth step came back in her voice he said that was four of his words and a half, and gave the cost: a lad of nineteen wrote his four steps into a book, a man off another route read it, and within about two years that depot had a poster with three steps and a photograph of a man in a lanyard, and the fourth step came off it entirely, and they were not doing it wrong, they were doing three quarters of it, faster, and it worked — **and the fourth step did not go missing in that depot, it went missing in about four hundred depots and there was no man in any of them who knew which one they had lost it in.** He keeps it on a timetable because a timetable goes out of date on a Sunday and nobody has ever been proud of a timetable. **And then a line cook of twenty-seven put a knife down and said nine words, once, without improving them and without explaining them: *You have taught a door to need a person.*** He agreed in about four seconds and said he was not going to argue with a cook about a door. **The three steps were not written down either, because a page with three quarters of a thing on it is the leaflet.** A schedule came round with the bell-push on it again as *external push, serviceable* on a new number, and a woman of about twenty-six said out loud in the room that it is the same number twice and that both of them were true on the day. **He will not be in that room on a Wednesday or a Monday, because his round starts at four in the morning and the room shuts at ten at night, and he will be at the market instead.**

### Chapter 0209 — "Four Feet Away"

**The Wednesday afternoon and evening of week seventy, from about ten to three to about ten at night, in a market in Saltmarket, and on a stair afterwards. Load-book entry 212, and its own opening label says this entry is a microphone on a stand and a rule that is true and a woman who said a thing twice. THIS IS THE ONE PANEL IN VOLUME 05 SO FAR.**

A trestle in a market put there by a company that does not know what is on it, holding a stall-holder's licence it uses about nine times a year, because a first-year of nineteen wrote one paragraph in the second week of week sixty-eight and signed it with neither a room nor a name. **A leaflet with one sentence on it and a name at the bottom of it in her own hand, put there on the Tuesday on purpose, having been told twice that a description of a room is a description of the people in it, and nobody can take it off, because there is no form on which a name can be taken off a leaflet and no mechanism in this city that can do it.** He is not a speaker and is not introduced as one; he is nine feet away with a bag of cable ties, and the first-year says so out loud and adds that it is on purpose. **A woman of thirty-four with a district jacket and a clipboard put the trestle there and would not say why in more than one sentence: the people who need a room like that are the people already standing in a market with a bag.** About forty people go past in an hour, about eleven stop, about four are spoken to, about seven take a piece of food and go on their way, and nobody is paid to be there. **A woman of about fifty-two with fish asked whether what people say in that room stays in that room, and he said nine words, counted on the page, which are true and do not answer her: *Nobody in that room can promise you that today.*** Then the apparatus, once, four feet from her, in two plain lines: *The condition stops you doing the thing. It does not stop anybody asking you about it.* **It is true, it answers nothing, it is the sixth time in two hundred chapters and the only one that has appeared where somebody was asking him a question, and he looked at the rule instead of at her face.** She said one more sentence and he did not hear it. He said sorry. She said it again once and slower: **there is a woman in this city who has been waiting since the spring because nobody told her a man could refuse to come, and she is not going to say her name in a market and has not said it to the girl on the table either.** Nobody wrote it down. **A nurse of twenty-four was nine feet away for about eleven minutes and did not intervene**, and gave the reason on a stair afterwards, and it is not forgiveness and it is not coldness: somebody would have stepped in, and then the thing that went out of that table would have been hers, and a nurse saying a room is careful is worth about half of a cook saying it. **The bus inspector from Chapter 208 stopped at the trestle and said out loud that this is a sentence somebody has already printed.**

### Chapter 0210 — "What The Room Will Not Do"

**The Thursday evening of week seventy, from about six to about ten, in four rooms off a service road, and at ten that night at a bench in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 213, and its own opening label says this entry is nine lines on a board, in six hands, and a seventh name.**

A piece of hardboard the size of a tea tray went on the wall inside the front room, under the sign-writer's board, not screwed to anything, about nine lines in six hands, no date, no number, no reference, nothing at the top. **Two things had happened in nine days and neither was planned: a woman of about thirty came back three times after coming once because it was raining and had been in the room about six hours across three visits; and a market acquired a sentence — eleven people stopped, a leaflet went out with a first-year's name on it, and on the Thursday morning a woman telephoned the number on the plate outside the unit and was put through to a letting clerk who said her sentence about a vacant frontage to a fifth set of people who did not know that was what she was doing.** A first-year of nineteen — **who is Asha Reed** — said the wall was needed because on Tuesday nobody could tell afterwards whether a woman had been in the room and on Wednesday about seven people who do not know them got a piece of food off a table, and those are two rooms about four feet apart — **and a wall is less than a form, and she has had that argument twice this week and lost it twice.** The nine lines were written out loud one at a time and a line was not a line until the person who lived it said it, and the first is a cook's, said with her hands in a bowl of water: **it will not keep anything it cannot take back.** Two lines are the cook's, two a first-year's, two a woman of thirty-one's, one a bus inspector's, one a man of twenty-nine's and one a printer of fifty-eight's, and the printer's is four words — *it will not be printed* — and the cost she named out loud for it is a hundred and forty-one pounds under a till that cannot be sent back. **The person not asked to sign is the woman of about thirty, and the reason was given out loud in the room and given back by her: if a wall has her name on it then in about four years the only line anybody reads out is a person who was in a room because it was raining.** Nobody asked her twice and the pen went past her. **And he signs it, having written none of the nine, and says out loud once that he is the only one of the seven anybody in this city has learned how to find in about three weeks, that the other six are at a counter, a shop four feet wide, a depot, a first floor with a key in it and a kitchen and one of the six is on nine Fridays a week across four employers, and that if his name is not on that wall then somebody has to be in that room every day of the week and neither he nor a woman with a ring file has met the person who does that.** He added that putting his name somewhere findable on purpose is the bill, and that the bill came due in a room off a service road instead of a corridor and he did not choose which. **And the last thing said in that room is a first-year of nineteen's: it is much smaller than we wanted it to be, and she wants that written down somewhere, and she is not writing it on the wall because it is not a thing the room will not do. About eleven people a week are still going to be taught that a release waits for a man in a lanyard, and a name on a wall by a door is not a rota either, and nobody in that room said otherwise.**

---

# VOLUME 04 — MOVEMENT V (Chapters 191–200), "The Ledger" — COMPLETE, AND VOLUME 04 IS CLOSED

**`outline/volume-04.md` governs and it was not re-argued. The load-book run is 194 to 203, continuous, one entry per chapter, no duplicate and no gap, and it ENDS HERE, which is the end of Volume 04. Nine of the ten entries are about somebody who is not Marek Senn, and the one that is on him is entry 203, which is the close. The eight-word overlap of each entry with its own chapter, measured on the files as they stand with the matcher described in `workspace/volume-04/batch-0005/SUMMARY.md`, is 23, 29, 21, 2, 15, 38, 29, 52, 41 and 49 — a mean of 29.9 and a range of 2 to 52, the lowest of the five movements in this volume and well inside the 38-to-284 family Volume 03 ran in. The dated hand-off to Volume 05 is `workspace/volume-04/batch-0005/VOLUME-CLOSE.md` and it supersedes this block.**

### Chapter 0191 — "One Over The Hundred"

**The Monday morning of week sixty-four, from about seven in the morning to about ten past ten, in a yard and a hut and a plant room behind the old continuity hall in Crown Terrace, and in a service corridor of the same building. Load-book entry 194, and its own opening label says this entry is an eleven-week item on a maintenance schedule, a strip of tape across the corner of a bundle, a line in a disposal book in a hut, and about nine hundred pages going over a wall into a container.**

A maintenance schedule in Crown Terrace running on eleven weeks; a strip of brown tape across the corner of a bundle at about seven; a line into a disposal book in a hut at about half past seven; about nine hundred pages over a wall at nine; eleven containers on a service road. **Every part of that is correct, and that sentence is the whole of what a term and a half has been arriving at, and nobody says it out loud, because a correct thing does not need saying.** Petar Vance, a field service engineer of fifty-one who services the exchange rooms and prints the plates, is in the plant room behind that wall with a tool bag because a heating circuit has been losing pressure since Friday and nobody has written one word about it. A man of about sixty counts nine bundles with steel weights, a clipboard and a roll of tape, and bundle nine is a hundred and six, and **a hundred and seven sheets are in it, because he counted a hundred and six, wrote it on the corner of the top sheet, and taped the corner, and both operations are correct and either one on its own puts that sheet in, and a bundle of a hundred and six with a hundred and seven sheets in it is not an error anybody can see from the outside. Nobody weighed it and nobody was going to.** A page came out flat on the concrete, face up, when four sheets went over on a corner: a service sheet, about the same age as a school exercise book, with a location in the top corner described in words and not by a room number, eleven dated marks down a printed column, all the same mark, and nothing on the back. **He is the only person in this city who can say what a plate belongs to; about nine hundred of the plates in four buildings came off one line of his own company's list and carry a number off a different line of the same list; he cannot change nine hundred doors because a district nurse wants one room, and he named that as the largest thing he has ever been responsible for and a list he did not write.** Chamber 3 is in no plan he has seen, a plan of the tunnel stops at the first chamber, and no company in this city holds a contract for anything in a tunnel, because a tunnel is under a building and a building is a landlord. **He put the page back, took no photograph, made no copy, and wrote four words and a week in the margin of his own book, which is the first entry in four years in it that is not a part.** He said out loud, to a man with a docket, that the reason he put it back is that a page in a pocket in a van tonight would be the whole of it, and in about four years there would be a man in a shed with a page in a drawer and no way of telling anybody why he had it.

### Chapter 0192 — "The Fifth Chair"

**The Wednesday morning and night of week sixty-four, from about half past nine in the morning to about ten past midnight, in a back room with the shutters up. Load-book entry 195, and its own opening label says this entry is forty-four lines, a fifth chair, and a number of telephone calls that did not move. THIS IS THE EXCHANGE.**

A back room, shutters up, a table, a tin with no lock, a book with four columns and forty-four lines, four minutes an item, no vote for anybody. **This is the fifth Wednesday on which the book has not been opened, counting from the Wednesday of week forty-eight, and the sittings are four weeks apart, so that there are three Wednesdays in between those five and four gaps between them, and the sixth falls four weeks from this day, outside Volume 04.** Ten people in the room, two of the twenty-nine who have telephoned her. **She said the number out loud and it has not moved in four weeks, and she said why: she has not telephoned anybody in four weeks, the last time she rang anybody it was the fortnight after a Wednesday in the spring and it was a frightened woman and she was the only person in this city who knew that woman had rung, and she cannot tell whether the second call was a habit or the third was the thing she was afraid of. She offered to ring one and did not.** The rule in one go: **a book of what a caller said cannot be written by the people who did not call.** The fortieth line is three years old, put there by a man who rang, said nine words and signed under them, and there has been no line since; line forty-four has a hole in it where a woman of sixty-eight in bay four ought to be, and she was never written down and she is not going to be. Two items and neither went in the book: a man of forty-four who wanted a room, got a sentence, and said that in about four years somebody will be able to say that about forty people asked for a binder and that the number who asked for that one is smaller than the number who asked for a different one, which is not true and which he knew was not true and which was the reason he could say it out loud; and a woman of about thirty who asked whether a caller who did not come ought to be written down, and was told that no rule exists for that half and none will be made at eleven at night in a full room. **The fifth chair was in the row, put there at about half eight by a woman of thirty-one with a key, a diary and no standing, and her reason is that a chair against a wall is not an answer to anything and a chair in a row is, and she has not got a rule for who is expected, and the rule about who may be in the room is the rule about the book and she is not going to split it into two so that she has one. She sat down in it and did not mean to.** Bea Nunn put it back against the wall herself, at the front, with both hands, and said the fifth thing she has said out loud in that room in a term and that none of the five is a rule she made up tonight. Nobody wrote anything down, the tin went back empty, and the book did not open.

### Chapter 0193 — "The Fourth Location"

**The Friday of week sixty-four, from half past seven in the morning to ten at night, at a depot, on a landing in a block off a service road, in a basement with a trolley, and at a bench in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 196, and its own opening label says this entry is a window that was replaced correctly, a woman of forty-six with a ring file, and a sentence a man gave two people on two Fridays in nine weeks.**

Eleven jobs, nine in date, grade three from eight, the word at nine a minute until about one and at ten after one, nobody asked him where a release was going to come from, and it is not a number he keeps. **The fourth location on a docket of his own in his own shorthand and on nothing else is no longer in that building:** the stair window's frame was measured on the Monday, ordered and fitted on the Wednesday in about four hours by two men and a boy, because the old frame was soft at the bottom; the frame came out whole; **the groove somebody cut into the inside of the reveal about forty years ago, two fingers wide, painted over since the summer of the fifty-fifth, went out with the old frame, and nobody who did the work knew it was there, and the man who ordered the window had put his hand on the old sill to test it and did not know, and the job sheet says the window was replaced and nothing else.** A man of thirty-eight in a district jacket said he has now been given two sentences from the same man on the same floor, identical, nine weeks apart, that he wrote the first one down because he was standing on the landing, and that he is not asking for a better reason; **the refusal is the same sentence word for word, about eleven jobs and a Friday, and it is not improved.** Then Ines Kolar, forty-six, in a basement with a trolley and a ring file: a woman of thirty-six who does the sheets up turned one over because everybody does, read fourteen lines of pencil, and asked what the pencil was, about four weeks before. **She has not asked again. The reason she has not answered: the day she answers, that woman is the person in this city who did not turn a sheet over, and in about four years somebody will want to know how she knew and the only version available will be a woman with a trolley.** The other half, said on a Friday in a basement: **there is no column on that sheet for what is on the door, there are forty-one lines and a column of room numbers and a column for conditions, a man of twenty-two asked her a question in a corridor in the week of the fifty-two and she put eleven more rooms in pencil in the space where the conditions go, the sheet went back on the trolley and went to a building and nobody has asked for it and nobody is going to, and she is not going to be asked to say his name to explain the pencil and will say what the pencil is and stop there.** The only record of the groove in this city is fourteen lines in pencil in a ring file in a cupboard nobody has ever opened.

### Chapter 0194 — "The Third Time"

**The Monday of week sixty-five, from about ten in the morning to about half past three in the afternoon, in a plant room, down a service tunnel, in a corridor, and in an office on the first floor of a building in Crown Terrace. Load-book entry 197, and its own opening label says this entry is a service tunnel, a steel hatch with no lock on it, and a third declination in writing with a count and a reason in it.**

A door at the back of a plant room that is on none of four plans; forty steps; a landing; **an old steel hatch with a lift ring, standing four inches open, with nothing on it and no lock on it — and a maintenance chamber in this city has never had a lock on anything, and the reason is structural rather than admirable, because the authority to lock a room is the same authority as the authority to say who goes in it, and that authority has never been written down anywhere in this city.** A man of about sixty with a trolley comes down that landing every Monday to see whether the hatch is where he left it. **Neither of them opened the hatch.** A man of twenty-two counted four doors on the way down and none of them was an exit and said the sentence that says a room is a room, and nobody was listening. **A contractor holding the cleaning and the paper contract for that building has offered in writing, for the first time in fourteen years, to have the table on the ground floor moved into a locked room on the first; the reasons given are insurance in the second paragraph and the impossibility of insuring a document in a corridor in the third.** Iven Sore, fifty-eight, a soft voice, a coat that has never been in a workshop, answered the first question with a smaller one, read the letter out in the first person, and declined it — the third of three, and he told them about all three, having told nobody about the other two. **The count: eleven weeks between rounds; nine hundred and six sheets across nine bundles on the Monday of week sixty-four; one bundle entered as a hundred and six and holding a hundred and seven; nine hundred and five sheets accounted for, the sixth not looked for and not going to be; one table; one bound register of about nine hundred lines in four columns with a job title in the fourth column of every line.** The reason, given the third time and different from the first two, which were about the end of the corridor: **a locked room is a room that things go into and do not come out of, the only thing that office has ever been good at is counting what goes in and what comes out, and a round that finds nothing is the same as a round that did not happen, and he would rather have nine hundred sheets in a skip than a building where nobody knows what was thrown away.** At the door he gave away the date of the next round — the Tuesday of next week, same round, same item, same tape, same line in a book in a hut — and said he did not know why he was telling them the date of a routine disposal, and that a count which is not given out loud is only a thing he has. **Nothing was agreed to, nothing was signed, no post was created, and the office retained everything it held on Monday morning.**

### Chapter 0195 — "Two People He Did Not Choose"

**The Thursday of week sixty-five, from about ten in the morning to ten at night, in a corridor, a service tunnel and a maintenance chamber under a building in Crown Terrace, at a counter, and at a bench in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 198, and its own opening label says this entry is a maintenance chamber under a building, a book in a drawer in it, and two people who read it who were not chosen by the man who went down. THIS IS THE VAULT AND IT APPEARS ONCE IN THIS VOLUME, AND SERIES MILESTONE FOUR IS PAID IN THIS CHAPTER.**

A woman of twenty-four is in that building because a mobile clinic's stock returns go to a counter there on Thursdays, and she went down a service tunnel with three other people because there were four of them and she was one of four, **and she said why before she was asked: a woman of forty-five had said no to her once and she would like to be in the room when she said it again, and that is not Marek Senn's business and not the other woman's either.** The chamber is called the Crown Vault on the plans that stop at it, is historical, and nobody in it used the name; it is about eleven feet by nine with a low ceiling, a steel stair, a painted grey cabinet the size of a wardrobe with a keyhole with no key in it, and about eleven conduit runs. **Nobody locked the hatch behind them because there is nothing on the hatch to lock.** In a shallow drawer in the wall is **a partial maintenance ledger, five columns, about the size of a telephone directory, running four years and ending about a year and a half ago with no notice, which is not how a book ends and is how one stops when nobody is writing any more.** The fifth column has a word at the top of it that means *who gave the word*, and it is blank in every line. **He got to the floor before anybody else and stayed on it and did not read it, and gave the reason: that he would have read it in nine seconds and brought back two words he liked and four hundred pages of somebody's arithmetic.** **Two people he did not choose read it between him.** Sera Quill, forty-five, turned the pages with two fingers and did not touch the writing, and stopped with her hand on a page and said **the nine words she has said for a term and a half, word for word, and the reason she has always given, which is a thing about a person in this city and not about a practice, and nobody in that chamber asked her a second question, and the name was not said.** Halla Rennick, forty-one, records clerk, twenty-two years, was asked in a corridor on the Friday before and said yes and said at the same time that she did not want to, and both were true; she read the columns out and said that the fifth column being blank in every line is the first one she has read in twenty-two years where the whole column is the point. A folded run sheet behind the last leaf, about forty lines, **nine of which are the same eleven words in the same order**, which is not an instruction. **Then he asked Talia Venn to carry the page, in front of both of them, on purpose, because he wanted a room to have heard it. She said no in nine words that are countable on the page: *I will not carry what I cannot answer for*. The refusal is about the page and not about him** — a piece of paper in a hand she cannot read goes into a coat on a bus and then into a room that is not hers, and in about four years there are two people who know what is on it and neither of them has got it, and being the person who happened to be in the room is a coincidence with a strap on, and she has been the cheapest person in a room more times than she can count in four years and has said no three of those times and this is the fourth — **and she picked up her bag off the stair and went to a counter that was opening at eleven.** **The records clerk put the leaf in her canvas satchel in front of four people on purpose and said she is signing nothing, that there is no slip and no form, and that if anybody asks her for it in a corridor in a fortnight the answer is a list of three people and it is not one. At the foot of the stair, in a corridor, Talia Venn told him he did the asking in front of them on purpose, told him not to call it trust, and called it the cheapest one in a tunnel.** He wrote down four words: *four people went down. One page came up. The one who is carrying it is not me.* **No name, no hand.**

### Chapter 0196 — "The Eleventh"

**The Friday of week sixty-five, the second Friday rota of this movement, from half past seven in the morning to about a quarter to midnight, at a depot and in a room in Crown Terrace, and at a bench in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 199, and its own opening label says this entry is the eleventh and last of eleven, thirty-one chairs, and a number an officer of a fund said out loud and did not minute.**

The Friday of week sixty-five is the last of the eleven. **Thirty-one chairs and fourteen people**, said out loud at a quarter to ten by Corin Vasska, forty-four, who has never minuted an attendance figure, and this is the lowest in four years. **His reason for not minuting it: a minute is a thing a committee reads and this committee does not meet again, and the number would be written for about nine people who will never be in a room together.** His reason for the attendance was given four Fridays earlier and was not given again, on the grounds that a reason given twice is a habit. **His second reason is about him: a series of eleven that ends because a man of forty-four read a sheet out loud eleven times is not a series, it is a habit, and he has been the habit, the eleven was a number somebody put in a specification section three years ago, the specification does not expire, the levy does not stop, the sessions stop, nothing in this city is going to write down what replaces them, and he has decided that is better than the alternative.** He read the third line out as the third line at about ten past ten, and about nine people in the room did not hear the difference, **which is the fifth time that has happened and the third time this term, and the line was not counted aloud.** **He did not read the five jobs off the front of the leaflet an eleventh time — about four hundred leaflets by the door, five jobs on the front of each, two of them his, put there by a system in a company out of two job sheets in the week of the thirty-fourth — having said in front of everybody four Fridays ago that he was not going to, with the reason that about eleven people a week are being taught to wait for a man and not one of those eleven has ever heard him read it. Nobody asked him to.** One man asked him to read the times and the fee aloud for people still coming through the door and was refused in one sentence. **A man of fifty-two, the depot manager, came at half past nine and left at about eleven and said nothing**, having said four Fridays earlier, standing, with a microphone on a stand, that he had stopped asking whether anybody was going to offer to take the wording of a condition to somebody who could change it; **a man of twenty-two in the fourth row noticed both facts and used neither, and coming was the whole of it.** A woman of twenty-nine, on the Friday list because an employer pays and not because anybody chose her, said that about four of the eleven people a week are not on the rota for that reason, and **refused to say what sixteen Fridays written on the back of her own card since the spring are for, because the day she writes what they are for it becomes a claim and a claim is a thing somebody can ask her to give up.** A woman of twenty-six at her table by the wall, at the eleventh of eleven sessions she has attended, having not stood in any of them, said the numbers out loud for the third time in a term: eleven letters, nine answers written, none sent, the ninth the best of the nine, two letters she is not going to answer at all, **and it is still not a decision, because there is nothing on the paper to answer it with.** **The standing problem was not said, nobody asked him anything, and no rule about who may be asked was invented.** Seventeen chairs were empty and the microphone was on its stand all night.

### Chapter 0197 — "One Over The Hundred, The Other Way"

**The Tuesday of week sixty-six, from twenty to seven in the morning to one in the afternoon, in a yard, a plant room, a service tunnel, a maintenance chamber and a corridor behind the old continuity hall in Crown Terrace. Load-book entry 200, and its own opening label says this entry is eight bundles, about nine hundred sheets, a strip of tape, a line in a disposal book, and a folded sheet that went the other way. THIS IS THE PURGE AND THE CARRYING-OUT.**

Half past six in the morning, a man of about sixty with a trolley, a clipboard, a roll of brown tape and a pair of steel weights, and a hatch standing four inches open. **Iven Sore said in his own corridor, in the first person, that he authorised the round: on the Monday, in writing, in one line, in a file in the only locked room in that building, and that the file does not know what is in them. His reason: a maintenance record has a life, the life is eleven weeks, a chamber under a building has no lock on it, and paper in a locked room is paper in a building nobody can count. And then the sentence that is not a defence: that he did not sleep badly about it, and that a man of fifty-eight telling a corridor that he did not sleep is a thing a corridor will hold for about four years.** The records clerk of forty-one was in the plant room with the leaf in her canvas satchel on a chair and **said out loud, in front of the man with the clipboard, at about ten past seven, that she was not putting it back in the drawer, and wanted it noted that she said no out loud on the day it was going.** The round took the drawer. **A folded sheet came out of it, and a man of about sixty unfolded it because it was folded, read it quickly and laid it flat on the top of the stack, which is also what a man does. There was no second reading, no check, no witness and no slip.** Eight bundles, about nine hundred sheets, eleven weeks, a count in a block hand on a corner, brown tape across that corner, a line in the disposal book in the hut — bundle, count, tape, the Tuesday, the item, and an authority column with a job title in it, because a job title is what a man can write without stopping to think about who he is. **Every part of it was correct, and the man is not a villain and was not asked to be careful and went home at about one.** Then the carrying out: up the steel stair, along a service corridor with a fire door at the end of it, forty steps, and then it stopped. **The records clerk stopped on a second landing, said she had got it, and gave one reason of five words — *because the last one did* — said once and not argued with. Nobody asked her who, she has never said, and the field service engineer said nothing about it then or afterwards, in a corridor, a canteen or a yard.** He took a canvas bag off a landing rail, checked that the bag was not his, and carried it the rest of the way out through a service door on foot; she stayed on the landing until the door had shut and then went down. **The bag belonged to a man who left that contract in the spring and whose locker nobody has cleared, and neither of them asked whose it was and both of them knew by the second landing that they were not going to. There is no mechanism that can make a person hand a bag back and no form on which a person can say whose bag it was, and the only thing either of them could have done was not pick it up. Nobody thanked anybody.** The last bundle went in at about twenty past and the count was said out loud to nobody. **About nine hundred and four of the sheets are about relay plates and cable runs and fire doors and a heating circuit, and every one of them was destroyed correctly. One of them is the only copy in this city, and a bag on a hook behind a door in a building is a thing that can be found by anybody who has a reason to be in a plant room, and a woman of thirty-nine said that in another city in another year, and everybody in that building knew it while it was happening, and nobody has ever written down that they knew it.**

### Chapter 0198 — "Fifty-Eight In A Tray"

**The Thursday morning of week sixty-six, from about half past nine to about a quarter to twelve, in a room off a street in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 201, and its own opening label says this entry is a tray with fifty-eight slips in it, nine of which have a person's name at the bottom, and a folder on a high shelf that is not a box.**

Thirty-one slips on the Friday of week fifty-nine, not one of them about a box, and **fifty-eight on this Thursday, counted and said aloud every Wednesday in between, the second time slower, and nine of the fifty-eight have a person's name written at the bottom of them, the first about a fortnight before this day, and there were none in the spring and none in the summer, and the nine are all different people, and none of the nine has been answered.** The other forty-nine are pieces of paper and she has never asked who put them in her tray and is not going to, because the day she asks, the room has got a column and the room does not have a column. **A slip with a person on it is a request and it goes in the tray; a slip without a person on it is a piece of paper, and there is no tray for that either.** Four of the nine came in between half past nine and about eleven, one at a time, and none knew the other three: **a foreman of fifty-eight with five lines of pencil in a site diary, a stranger's box number in his hand since the spring, and a telephone on a wall, asking whether he is allowed to keep writing them, and told that a diary is a diary and that what he is allowed to keep is in a document behind a counter and is not going to be guessed at; a woman of twenty-nine with sixteen Fridays on the back of a card and no total ever written, asking whether a card is a record, and told that it is a record if somebody keeps it and that nobody does; a care worker of thirty-eight, nights, six years, who came because a woman of thirty-four told her about the room once in a corridor, asking the single question whether a person can find out who has read a page, and told no with the reason — a box in Bower Street with about two hundred pages in four stacks and no system behind any of them, so there is nothing in it that could answer — and told that the difference between a no and a true thing is that somebody can be angry with a no, and that she did not sign the slip and the name on it was written by the woman of forty-one; and a printer of fifty-eight with four feet of shop in the Quiet Quarter and a hundred and forty-one pounds in an envelope under a till that she cannot move, asking whether a person can print something and then not be the person who decides about it, and told, correctly, that the registrar does not know the answer and would rather say so than say something that would be repeated in four years as though she had thought about it.** A folder went up onto the high shelf of the trolley on the Wednesday: **a slip is required for a box coming down off that shelf, a folder going up onto it is not that, and she is not going to start a record of what goes up, because a record of what goes up is a list, a list is a column, and the room does not have a column. She has not read it and nobody in the room has read it.** And the sentence, said once, at about a quarter to twelve: **you cannot ask for a binder nobody has told you about. About forty people in nine years have come through that door and every one of them asked for a binder they had been told about, and not one of them has ever asked for the one on that counter, and that is not because it is a secret, it is because nobody has ever said out loud that there is a second one.** Nothing was adopted, distributed, minuted or noticed; the tray is a drawer.

### Chapter 0199 — "Retained"

**The Monday of week sixty-seven, from about half past ten in the morning to ten at night, on the ground floor of a building in Crown Terrace and in the corridor above it, and at a bench in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 202, and its own opening label says this entry is a bound register on a table with nobody behind it, a man of fifty-eight in a corridor saying one sentence with a count in it, and an authority retained by a person.**

A bound register on a table on the ground floor of a building in Crown Terrace, canvas, four columns, about nine hundred lines, the oldest from the week of week thirty-eight, no rope and no sign, and **the fourth column is a job title in every one of about nine hundred lines and has never once had a person's name in it. Nobody has ever been on that floor on a Thursday.** A woman of thirty-four was behind it on this Monday with a cardigan, a cup of tea and a folder she had not opened; she said she had not asked him the question she had promised to ask and that she had noticed, and asked the second one, whether the register is going to be moved, saying before she asked that she is the frightened one and not him. **He answered neither way: the answer to it staying is not his and the answer to it going is a thing about a Tuesday, and two offices in that building learned that the answer to everything is yes until about four years ago. He said afterwards that it was not an answer and that he had given it anyway, because nobody had asked him anything for four years.** Then the one sentence with a count in it: **eight bundles in a yard behind that building on the Tuesday of week sixty-six, about nine hundred sheets into a skip, one of them a folded leaf that came out of a drawer because a man of about sixty unfolded it, carried out by a records clerk of forty-one on her own time and put on a high shelf in a room off a street in Lattice Ward; he has known that since the Wednesday; he is not going to ask for it; the number of people in this city who could tell him where it is is two, and neither of them works for him.** And the reason for not asking, which he asked her to say in her own voice so that it would be a thing a room had heard: **a page that has left a building is a page somebody is carrying, and the second that an office asks for a page is a man in a corridor with a piece of paper to hand to a person, and then the person is findable, and he has declined two things in writing in fourteen years and both of them were about not making a person findable, and he is not going to do it a third time in a fortnight.** A man of twenty-two on the landing at the foot of that stair asked one question, badly — does the requirement still stand — and was told that **it stands, it has stood the whole fourteen years, nothing in this city has ever withdrawn it, the only way it could be withdrawn is by somebody who can be asked whether they intend to, there is no such person, and nobody has ever asked him whether he is it; and that he could be, and would decline it in writing, and that the fourth column has held a job title instead of a name for fourteen years and has worked exactly as well as it looks.** Then, standing up, in a corridor, in the first person: **he is retaining emergency authority and is telling them so on a Monday morning and is not going to put it in a form**, because a form of that can be produced by anybody who has ever seen one, and in about four years somebody is going to write down that the Continuity Office retained emergency authority on a Thursday, it will be true, and it will be the only true sentence anybody will ever have about him, and he would rather it was said by three people in a corridor and that it is a man saying it and not a stamp. And the whole of his position in one sentence: **he has not seen nine requests, he has been told about a tray, he has been asked nothing, he has agreed to nothing and refused nothing, and those two are not the same, and there is nobody in this city who has to make them the same.** He wrote down the number three and no names.

### Chapter 0200 — "A Card In A Rack"

**The Thursday of week sixty-seven, from about eleven in the morning to ten at night, in a room off a street in Lattice Ward and at a bench in the same city. Load-book entry 203, and its own opening label says this entry is a card about the size of a playing card in a rail at the end of a counter, and the fact that it is renewable and removable and has no title and no office and nothing on the back of it. THIS IS THE CLOSE, THE VOLUME'S FINAL IMAGE IS REACHED ITEM BY ITEM, AND THE LOAD-BOOK RUN ENDS HERE AT 203.**

A rail of card slots at the end of a counter, about nine cards in it in nine years, a name on one side and a day on the other, and **the woman of forty-one who runs that room has never once taken one out and took one out on this Thursday with two fingers, slowly, which is the first time in nine years one has come out of that rail, and put it on the counter. She was not at work: she comes on Mondays, Wednesdays and Fridays, this is a Thursday, and she came on her day off to look at a folder she had put on a high shelf two days before, and said so before anybody asked.** Five people in that room — a registrar of forty-one on her day off, an officer of a fund of forty-four, a woman of forty-six with a trolley and nineteen years of a condition sheet signed at the bottom, a courier of twenty-one with three practices in three hands and a worse answer he prefers, and a man of twenty-two — and none of the other four was asked to be there and none knew the other three. **He put the card in at about eleven in the morning, in his own hand, on purpose, and on the worst possible day in the week, when there was no counter staff and no bell within reach of anybody's hand.** The front of it carries **a name in a hand that gets smaller towards the end of it and a week at the bottom left in the same hand, and nothing else: no title, no office, no second name, nobody's permission, anybody's agreement; a woman of thirty-one with a key to a room in another building did not agree to anything and is not on it.** He turned it over before he put it in, and there is nothing on the back of it. **It is renewable by him in his own hand without asking anybody, removable by a clerk because a clerk can take it out of a rail, and the entire mechanism is a drawer with a rail on the end of it.** Then the reason, out loud, once, to four people: **a ledger nobody may read is a ledger a person owns, and a ledger a person owns dies with the person** — a folder on the high shelf of that trolley, about forty lines on it in a hand, and he did not read it and neither did two of the four people in that chamber, and the only copy in this city is on a shelf in a room anybody can walk into, and if it stays with one person then the day that person stops coming it is in a drawer in a flat and then it is a skip, and he would rather it was a card in a rail in a room off a street. **An officer of a fund of forty-four said: that does not help** — and then said what the sentence actually does, that it is a true sentence and also a sentence that puts his name on the front of a piece of paper in a building with a plate on the outside of it, and that in about four years the only thing anybody in this city will be able to say about the case is where to find him, and about eleven people a week are being taught that a release waits for a man in a lanyard — **and asked him to do the arithmetic out loud, and he did, in about nine seconds: about eleven people a week will go on waiting, about four of the eleven are not even on a rota, none of it is because of anything he did, he has traded the last thing he had, a man of fifty-eight in a corridor told him a term and a half ago that a person who says a thing is a person who can be asked whether he said it, he was right, he did it anyway, and the arithmetic is his and he is not going to hand it to a fund. Nobody said it was the right thing.** A woman of forty-six with a trolley said the only question anybody has ever asked her that her own sheet could not answer was about a corridor in the week of the fifty-two, and that nobody thanked her then and nobody has since. **Then the woman of forty-one said she is not handing it to anybody who has not filled in a slip, in either direction, that the card is going in the tray with the fifty-eight, and that she is not starting with a man who is good at rooms** — the only thing anybody said in that room that the man of forty-four could not improve. **Then the custody of the sheet, said out loud: a high shelf in a room off a street, a plain folder, no card and no slip, put there on the Wednesday by a records clerk of forty-one on her own time; and the binder with the page stapled into it is a different object, is three shelves down, and is not to be put in a sentence with this one, because the day they are in the same sentence somebody will ask which one is a record and the answer will be whichever one a person is standing next to. A rack is not a rota, and nobody in that room said otherwise.** Then the bench, the drawer with five things in it, the jacket, the original in the fourth pocket of the tool roll with nothing on its fifth line, and the statement that the card in the rail and the master are two objects and were not confused today. Then the rest of it, item by item, and the last line: **about eleven people a week still going to be taught that a release waits for a man in a lanyard, and there is a rack now, and a rack is not a rota, and nobody in that room said otherwise.**

### The measured figures for Movement V, reproducible from the ten files with the strict matchers printed in `workspace/volume-04/batch-0005/SUMMARY.md`

**26,053 words. Prose 18,582 and load books 7,471. Raw instances of *about*: 310, 11.90 per thousand. True hedges: 211, 8.10 per thousand. Bold spans, strict matcher: 157, 6.03 per thousand, worst chapter 8.33. The ceilings of 10.75 and 12.4 in the batch prompt are the *bold emphasis* ceilings and only the bold figure is measured against them, and it is inside both; neither the raw count nor the true hedge is governed by a ceiling in this repository, and Chapter 197 is above twelve on both of those and breaches nothing.** The bold figure is the second lowest of the five movements of this volume and the true hedge the second highest, and both are stated rather than dressed up. Every line carries an even number of `**` and an even number of `"`; there is no `* * *` marker, no month-name, month-date, day-date, year or day number anywhere; there is no duplicated paragraph at an eighty-character threshold, and that clause is true only after the review repair pass added a within-batch pass, because the backwards sweep this batch relied on cannot see two of its own ten files sharing a paragraph — it found a 319-character duplicate between Chapters 196 and 200, and three more pairs at 66 to 84 characters, and all four are reworded; **the words *pattern*, Iona, Sorn, the narrator's *Quiet House*, *variant*, and the four words *we*, *our*, *us* and *rack* outside Chapter 200 appear nowhere in the ten files, which is checked mechanically; the word *ledger* appears only in Chapters 195 and 200; and the fourth condition is not quoted anywhere in the batch.**

### The eighty-character sweep against all one hundred and ninety earlier chapters

**26 maximal shared runs of eighty characters or more, resolving into 24 distinct strings, the largest 121, a mean of 86.6 — the lowest count of the five movements of this volume, against Movement IV's 38 runs, 32 distinct and a largest of 144. The sweep method was re-verified in this batch's review repair pass by running it on Movement IV's own ten files first, where it returns the published largest of 144 exactly and a run count of 34 against the published 38, and that difference is a degree of freedom in how a run is split when a string sits in more than one earlier chapter and is disclosed in the batch summary rather than averaged away.** Every remaining run is canon the brief requires word for word, the volume's inherited refrains, or the load-book house frame, and **nineteen blockers were reworded, four of them in the review repair pass, every one of them this batch's own framing prose and not a refrain.**

### The two things a later writer must not undo in this block

1. **VOLUME 04 IS CLOSED AND THE LOAD-BOOK RUN IS FINISHED AT 203.** The run is continuous from entry 50 at Chapter 1 to entry 203 at Chapter 200, with no duplicate and no gap, and it is not extended by any later volume's entry. **A Volume 05 writer begins at Chapter 201 and its first load-book entry is 204, and the pattern of one entry per chapter on the days fixed in that batch's prompt is inherited.**
2. **BOTH RE-BASES ARE SPENT AND NEITHER MAY BE SPENT AGAIN.** The relay was staged once, in Chapter 136, and there is none in any of Volume 04's fifty chapters. The recognition is a card in a rail, in his own hand, with a week on it, renewable, removable by a clerk, with no title, no office and nothing on the back, and it costs him not being findable. **There is no title on anybody in this volume, no office, no post, and no name on the front of any document in the whole of it except the one name he wrote himself on a piece of card about the size of a playing card.**

### Chapter 0141 — "The Cheapest Way To Close A Room"

**The Monday of week 42, from about two in the afternoon to half past five, in a room above a print works in Crown Terrace. Load-book entry 144.** The entry is about a man of forty-four from a levy who has found out in nine days what a name on a document is for, and nine people who did not agree with him, and forty people who have written in since the spring.

### Chapter 0142 — "Nine, Ten And Eleven"

**The Friday of week 42, from about eight in the morning to ten at night. Load-book entry 145.** The entry is about a woman of twenty-nine with nine hundred lifts behind her who has said a number out loud in a canteen because nobody asked her a question, and about a car park with nothing in it.

### Chapter 0143 — "A Number And Not An Address"

**The Monday of week 43, from half past seven in the morning to ten at night. Load-book entry 146.** The entry is about a foreman of fifty-eight with a carpenter's pencil and a tray that goes in a skip, and about a sentence he has had in a book in a hut since the summer and has never shown anybody.

### Chapter 0144 — "Not A Group"

**The Wednesday of week 43, from about two in the afternoon to ten at night. Load-book entry 147.** The entry is about a woman of thirty-five who checked the door on the way out, and about a man of forty-four who has wanted to be asked a question for four years and asked one and was refused in four seconds.

### Chapter 0145 — "The Bottom Of The Page"

**The Friday of week 43, from half past one in the afternoon to ten at night. Load-book entry 148.** The entry is about a woman of twenty-four who has two hundred and six pages and has not added one in a term and a half, and about a drawer in a converted shop unit with a page in it, and about a man of thirty-four with two cards who told me the other one is better.

### Chapter 0146 — "Four Feet Of Shop"

**The Tuesday of week 44, from about ten past two in the afternoon to half past five, and then a telephone call at ten past five. Load-book entry 149.** The entry is about a printer of fifty-eight with four feet of shop and a hundred and forty-one pounds in an envelope with no name on it, and about a boy of nineteen who asked the only question anybody in this city has asked about the two of them.

### Chapter 0147 — "A Ledger Is Not A Book"

**The Wednesday of week 44, from about half past seven in the evening to ten to midnight. Load-book entry 150.** The entry is about a woman of sixty-one with a pen who said a rule out loud at ten to midnight for eleven seconds, and about three hands on one sheet of typing paper and a fourth column of nobody's.

### Chapter 0148 — "The Same Shape"

**The Friday of week 44, from about half past six in the evening to a quarter to eleven. Load-book entry 151.**

### Chapter 0149 — "A Box In Pencil"

**The Wednesday morning of week 45, and then the afternoon of it, on a counter on the ground floor of a building in Bower Street. Load-book entry 152.**

### Chapter 0150 — "What Is Left On The Shelf"

**The week of week 46, Monday to Saturday, and the close. Load-book entry 153.**
