# VOLUME 04 — MOVEMENT II (Chapters 161–170), "The Reading Room" — COMPLETE

**`outline/volume-04.md` governs. The load-book run is 164 to 173, continuous, one entry per chapter, no duplicate and no gap. All ten entries are about somebody who is not Marek Senn, and the two that come nearest him are the close (173) and one section of it. The eight-word overlap of each entry with its own chapter was 84, 136, 130, 147, 120, 144, 168, 155, 233 and 206 as first published, a mean of 152 and a range of 84 to 233, and the live figures after the review repair pass are 96, 160, 155, 174, 155, 188, 218, 190, 255 and 236, a mean of 183 and a range of 96 to 255, all inside the 38-to-284 family with none above it, and no entry has needed a repair pass although Chapter 169 moved from 233 to 255 when its load book was turned from restated dialogue into records.**

### Chapter 0161 — "The First Question"

**The Thursday morning of week fifty-one, a quarter to ten to twenty past ten, in a corridor and then at a table on the ground floor of a building in Crown Terrace. Load-book entry 164, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is a woman of thirty-four who has read a column out loud for four years and has been asked about it once, by me, with the right word, three weeks after she told me to use it.** Doreen Abbiss came in the daylight on purpose and had been awake since five. **She refused to do it in a corridor in eleven seconds and made him ask it at the table.** The whole of the difference is one word and she had told him the difference at a threshold on the Tuesday of week forty-eight: he asks what she thinks of the fourth column, not what is in it, and she made him say both to prove he knew the difference. **Imogen Bray, thirty-four, four years at that table, about four hundred read-outs, asked twice about the column and told once that it is how the register is written, has never once been asked the second question.** Her answer in three parts: the only column anybody has ever read out loud, and nobody has ever gone quiet while she read one; a job title in a column is a sentence with the person taken out of it, and nine people a year have seen it and none has written to her; and **four hundred people have had it read out and not one has come back and said what they thought it was, and she cannot tell whether that is agreement or a queue.** She said the cost out loud, unasked. She gave her name into the table and said it is not going on the register, and said a name on a register is fourteen years of a column with a job title in it and a name in a room is a Tuesday. **The second question was the woman of thirty-eight's: has anybody ever been anything for a line in that register, and the answer is no in fourteen years, and nobody has been praised for one either because there is no line in it with a name on it, and she said that is worse than the one she came for.** On the stairs he did not thank her, because she told him on a half landing at twenty past midnight that he was not going to and that she was not going to be thanked, and she said the cost has arrived and that writing things down is what he does and it is a good thing to be and it is not a use.

### Chapter 0162 — "The Whole Of The Condition"

**The Friday of week fifty-one, half past two to about ten to three, in a corridor and on a stairwell of a block off a service road, on grade three. Load-book entry 165, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is a woman of thirty-four with a file with her name at the top of it, who had been saying a condition wrong to about six people since the spring, and who has not written it down.** Eleven jobs, nine in date, the word at nine and then at ten, and nobody asked him where a release was going to come from, which is four Fridays. He did the distribution board, which is the half of a callout that decides whether the light comes on, and did it well, and the lamp came on behind him in the lobby. **A district nurse of thirty-four asked him to say the fourth condition word for word so that she could check her own copy, and he put the four names in before *at any time*, and she stopped him at the word *this*, and both of them had it wrong in the same place, hers copied off a sheet in a room in Crown Terrace in about four seconds with about nine people in it.** The order is the only part of that sentence that matters and he cannot hold it after two o'clock and has not been able to since the Friday of week forty-seven, when he wrote two sentences down in the wrong order and the wrong order made a man who had come to help sound like a man who had come to complain, and the note he wrote that day said ask him again or do not use it, and then he decided the note was not right either. **She said why a condition must not get a review on it: if it had an expiry, or a review, or a person's opinion, then a person could wait for it to lapse, and a file with a name at the top of it is a corridor somebody stands in with a clipboard waiting for a date. She did not write it down, because a piece of paper with a condition on it is a thing somebody can ask for.** A depot manager of fifty-two came up nine floors, which he does not do, to put the wording to somebody who could put it to somebody, and was told no before the reason was asked for and was given the reason anyway: the moment somebody starts looking at the wording it becomes a thing that could be worded differently, and the only thing that has kept it honest for a term and a half is that it is a sentence nobody can improve. He said it was the same thing she had said four minutes earlier, nine words apart, and that neither had heard the other, and that this is now twice this term. **The sheet stays as it is.** He said the whole of it once on a fourth landing to nobody, in the right order, in about eleven seconds, on his way down.

### Chapter 0163 — "Sixteen Weeks On A Card"

**The Sunday before and the Monday morning of week fifty-two, four miles on foot, in a corridor on the ninth floor of a tower block in Nine Bridges and then in a motor room. Load-book entry 166, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is sixteen Fridays on the back of somebody's own card, given to me on purpose, by a woman of twenty-nine, because I am the only person in this city who can say a number out loud and I am not allowed to be asked about one.** **BOTH RELATIONSHIP BEATS ARE PAID HERE AND NOWHERE ELSE IN THE MOVEMENT.** Milestone 1, reported and not staged: on the Sunday he walked four miles to a converted shop unit with a folding screen and nine chairs and a district leaflet on a pinboard he has never seen taken down, and he did not go in, and four miles away a woman of twenty-four spent the one clean call she has in a week on a counter in Bower Street and said a district and nothing else, and **neither of them has ever mentioned the other's Sunday to the other and there has been nothing to mention for a term and a half.** Milestone 2: she was in that corridor for her own round, said **three weeks**, said she had not said anything about it and was not going to, and then said nine words — **you have made somebody askable who was not askable** — told him it is nine words and that it did not get better, said she had two visits and a bag and a lift that is out, **did not say she told him to do it, and went down the corridor and into a flat.** No scene, no declaration, no kiss, no *we*, the bench not reopened, Chapter 102's Tuesday not revisited, the Chapter 7 page closed. **Then the motor room.** Marguerite Okpara, twenty-nine, six years, about nine hundred lifts, on the Friday list because her employer pays for the card, and the card says eleven. **She had a piece of cardstock cut off something with a printed edge on the back of the card with sixteen lines on it in her own hand in biro, two numbers to a line, and she did not hand it over — she put it on the machine housing face down and then turned it round and stood with her back to the door, which she said afterwards she had done on purpose.** The card says eleven on all sixteen and the buildings had eight, nine, nine, ten, nine, eleven, nine, eight, ten, nine, nine, eleven, nine, eight, ten and nine, and she did not total them because a total is a number somebody can quote. There is a fortnight at eleven twice and she checked it twice. **She showed it to him on purpose, and the reason is that he is the only person who can say a number out loud in a room and is not allowed to be asked about it, so giving it to him is the only route that is not a route.** Her cost, ready since the Monday: the back of her own card, her employer on the front, no copy, no form in this city that takes a number like that, and if that card is in a pocket in Lattice Ward for a term and a half then in about four years the sixteen lines are gone. **He did not write a line of it on the docket, and she watched him not write it, and the reason he gave is that the word on the back of that docket is nine, ten and eleven a minute and it did not stop him hearing a voice and it did not stop him asking a question about one, and he has already cost one person a name by writing on the right piece of paper.** It is in her head and his, and a man of thirty-four who works nights has counted it too, which makes three, and he has told neither of them. She said she had not learned anything and had not been asked a question and was going home at the same time as usual, and when he said she had learned something she said no: I have got a list and I cannot use it and she has got a list and she cannot give it away, and the two of us are the only people in this city who know that, and in about four years one of us will be asked about it and one of us will not.

### Chapter 0164 — "A Note In His Own Hand"

**The Tuesday morning of week fifty-two, about ten past four in the afternoon in a stairwell in Nine Bridges, four hours before he starts. Load-book entry 167, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is nine words a man of thirty-four wrote on the back of a work card in the spring, in his own hand, that have stopped working, and a no he cannot get a hand on.** He asked for the stairwell and not the canteen and had said why in the week of week forty-seven over a tray of milk jugs. **He has nine words in biro about a doorway in Nine Bridges in the spring that he had said out loud to two men, and a man who had gone, and about four stairwells since, none of them the right one, and it is what stopped him saying that anybody handing you the other sort of card is a front, and he can still hold that.** What he cannot hold is the second refusal: **in the week of the thirty-eighth a woman with a lanyard asked him to take the word on his own floor for his own four people so that they would have somebody to wait for, and she was pleasant about it and gave him four minutes, and he said no in a second and a half. He has tried the reason out loud four times in a stairwell and once on a bus and the sentence is different every time and none of them is the one from the spring, and there is not a second reason, there is a door with something behind it he cannot get a hand on, and he stopped trying in about a fortnight because he was getting angry about it and angry is how he got the first one wrong.** He asked which of the two he has got, a reason with the words gone or no reason, and was told he is not on the card at all, that there is a job title in about four hundred pay packets in one district and it is a man of twenty-two, and that the number is about eleven people a week and it is not a rate. **The sentence that was available and was not taken: he could have said it, out loud, in the man's own words, out of his own book, and it would have worked in a corridor and in a canteen and in that stairwell and nobody would have known, and that is demonstrating, and he did it in his head in about nine seconds and told the man he had done it and said he was not going to do it, and the reason is that a sentence of his in a stairwell is not a reason and in four years it will be the only thing in this city saying the man was right.** He wrote a number down wrong twice and the man said it three times and asked for that to be on the record. At about twenty past four he said what he had come to say: he is keeping both cards, is not asking anybody to take either off him, has been asked twice since the spring by people with lanyards to hand his card in and take a company one, has said no twice, has a reason for the first no and not for the second, **and wanted it noticed that the no came first both times, and said he wants it kept and not used, and that there is a difference and nobody in this city has a word for it and he is not asking for one.**

### Chapter 0165 — "Eleven Who Asked First"

**Eleven at night on the Wednesday of week fifty-two, and again at twenty past midnight, in a back room. Load-book entry 168, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is a tin that is not locked and a book that was not opened for the second time in six weeks, and a woman of sixty-one who has said yes eleven times to a question she did not know she was answering.** Nine people, a tin with no lock on it, a book with four columns and forty-four lines in it that has not been opened since the Wednesday of week forty-eight, and a fifth chair against the wall, empty since the spring, not moved, and one person offered to move it and was told the chair is not the room's business. **She said the rule in one go and it is a rule and not a mood and it is older than her and was there before the tin: a book of what a caller said cannot be written by the people who did not call. You can say anything, all of it, in your own words, in whatever order it comes out, and nobody will write it down and nobody will stop you, and there is no rule in that room against talking; the rule is against the book.** A woman of thirty-one who had read a leaflet and had rung the number twice to ask whether it was true was told that if that is right she is not a caller and the argument was not going to be had, and she used three of her four minutes and none of it was written down. **She asked whether a person who says something in that room in their own words with nobody writing it down is protected, and said a woman with a lanyard had told her at a counter in about four seconds that a practice can write about itself in the first person and nothing else can, and that she had come four miles for that, and was told it means what it says and that nobody in nine years has asked her what it means.** A man of thirty-four used two of his four minutes on a thing he had said in a stairwell the day before and asked out loud that it not go in the book; he was thanked for saying it in the room rather than afterwards, was not asked whether he would ring on a Sunday, and said he would not. **And then the number, unasked: in about three weeks eleven people have telephoned her to ask whether it is true that a person can say anything in that room, and she has said yes eleven times, and she did not know what she was saying yes to.** Her own account of the risk: a room where a person can say anything becomes a room where a person comes to be told something, and being told something by a woman behind a counter at eleven at night in a room with no job in it is worth a very great deal to somebody who has been frightened for about four months, and she has not got a rule about that, because the other half of her rule is that she does not decide who is a caller. A man of thirty-four told her that half is not a rule, and she agreed, and said that is exactly why it must not sound like one. **The book was not opened. Nobody signed. Nobody called.**

### Chapter 0166 — "Four Letters And A Counter"

**About eleven in the morning on the Thursday of week fifty-two in a prefab office off the Meridian corridor with a second gate lamp open, and for about two hours afterwards. Load-book entry 169, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is a telephone on a wall that has been there since before the mobile came, a stock clerk of thirty-one with a field on a return that has not changed in four quarters, and a counter in a town with four letters in it where a woman will not say a name.** **A stock clerk called Rowan Teale, thirty-one, rang it**, because a box he fills in on a stock return every quarter has held the same four-letter post town and the same box number for four quarters and he was told to re-verify a field that had not changed before he could submit it, and there was nobody at his end to ask. Bryn Maw told him the yard is the return point if it is on the docket and the docket is the company's; that the box is not his; that he has got a stranger's box number in a stranger's handwriting and a town with four letters in it; **that he is the second holder of it; and that there is one other and he is twenty-two and is not there**, and added that he was not going to ask the man for him and the man said he was not going to offer it. **Then the clerk did the ordinary thing and rang the town, and a recorded voice gave the office, the day and the hours, and said that to collect from a box you must quote the box number and the reference from the card.** A woman at the counter called Bettina Rook in a town called Brin, which is four letters and is not Nacre, wrote nothing down because there is no place at that counter to write a stranger's box number down, and said: it is real, it is on the end wall third bay, it has got a card on it, the card is the only record of who holds it, the card is not something I can look at for a man on the telephone, and I am not going to tell you who holds a box and I would not do it if you were standing here. **About four hundred boxes in that building and about nine in that town that anybody collects from, and the rest are company names, and a company name is not a person. And then, unasked and before the line went: it has not been collected from in about two years, because I empty the row on a Friday and I have for eleven years.** The cost, twice, in his own words, and then the part that cost him: a telephone on that wall is the only addressable thing about him in this city, he chose it on purpose in the spring and told Marek Senn so, **and a man at a company has now heard a stranger's box number said out loud in that office with the door propped, twice.** **He did not want the call and did not say so for about two hours, and gave the bad reason himself and said he was giving the bad one: said to a man at a company it would have been a sentence about a company, the man has a form with a field in it, and in about four years there is a note in a file about that office, and he has watched about four companies put a thing in a file that should have gone in a man's head and said that in the spring and is not taking it back. Said to Marek Senn is different because that book is not a system and has no reference on it, and he said that is the same shape as the thing he would not say and that he does not have a better account of it than that.** **The field is still blank on three documents in three buildings and on a company's own stock docket and nobody has been told the name of anything.**

### Chapter 0167 — "What Is On The Hall Table"

**The Friday of week fifty-two, from about half past six to a quarter to eleven, at the eighth of the eleven sessions in a room in Crown Terrace. Load-book entry 170, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is a card on a hall table in Nine Bridges and a woman of forty-three who asked the only question anybody asked, and the sixth time the standing problem has been said in public, and this is the first time the man it belongs to said it himself.** Forty folding chairs, nine of them empty, a microphone on a stand, four hundred leaflets by the door, five jobs on the front of which two are him. **The woman of twenty-six who types things was at her table and did not stand up.** The man in the district jacket who wrote down three answers in the fifth row on the Friday of week forty-eight was not in the room, and there was nobody in the fifth row. **A woman of forty-three, who gave her name and where she works before she asked, asked twice because the first was quick: there is a card on a hall table at one of the three addresses she works at, four steps and a fifth line and her employer's name at the top, the leaflet says what the fifth line is for, is that about her. She said the reason she was asking him was that he is the only person on that sheet who is a person, and that is the first time anybody in this case has said that out loud about a list of jobs.** The answer was nine words and it did not answer her, and then the shape of what he has got, and then the one thing he has: the fifth line is on the card and not in somebody's head, and if nobody comes, put it down and say so. **He then stood up in the fourth row and said the condition himself, in the first person, for the first time in a term and a half of letting other people say it about him, and the microphone was on a stand and nobody asked for it to be switched off and nobody in this city can.** The six reasons are the six reasons and no rule about who may be asked was invented. **She put her hand up, which nobody expected, and asked who is allowed to ask him about this, was told nobody can tell him that, and said then I am allowed, I am a member of the public and I have read your leaflet and I have asked you a question in a room with a microphone on it, nobody here can stop me, and I would like that written down somewhere and I do not think it can be, and I would like somebody to try. Nobody tried.** And then her own cost, out loud, unasked: she said a name and a street out loud in a room of thirty-odd with a microphone on a stand, the address she works at is on a leaflet in nine hundred rooms, and there is a hall table with a card on it in that house and she goes in that house four days a week, **and it cost her a hall table, and she would say it again and would also like it not to have been a microphone.** A woman of twenty-six wrote nothing down and said why, and would not tell the room which of those two things matters more because she does not know.

### Chapter 0168 — "The Thing About The Call"

**The Monday morning of week fifty-three, about eleven, in a corridor and afterwards. Load-book entry 171, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is nine seconds on a telephone, made by a district nurse of thirty-four who has her own name at the top of her own file, and a man of fifty-one who said the thing about the call to the first person he has been able to say it to.** Roy Amiss is fifty-one with a hip and a fortnight of stairs in him, and about four months have passed since a building in this city had him for a leg over four weeks in the middle of a run of Thursdays and gave him back a printed card for his own table and a line for his employer in somebody's list of things that went right. **Nobody rang him before or after, and he said so out loud in a repair shop in the week of week forty-six to the only man in it, and said he was not saying it to anybody, and went out with the leaflet and the door took a while, and that is seven weeks ago.** **A district nurse of thirty-four rang him from a corridor at about eleven, and she is not Marek Senn and is not Talia Venn, and it took about nine seconds, and she wrote nothing down before the call and nothing down after it.** She said who she was, that she is the person who did not ring him, that she has been not ringing him since the Thursday, that she has not got anything to give him this morning, and that she was going to put the phone down and he would not be able to have the conversation and that is correct. **He said the thing about the call, which he had had ready since the week of week thirty-four and had practised on a bus: he read the card on the Thursday night, waited a week, nobody rang, he has not rung anybody either, he has a phone that works and a hip and a fortnight of stairs, and for about three weeks the only thing he has done with any of it is wait for a telephone.** He said he was not going to say thank you and gave the reason, and said he wanted the other half noticed. **He refused to be comforted and said so and said he would like the rest of it written down in whatever the nearest thing to a record is, which is this, and was told there is no record of it, and said then he has said it into a telephone and a nurse of thirty-four heard it, and that is the whole of the record and it is in a corridor and not in a system, and he has got a worse one of those than a leaflet.** She did not fill in the district's form for people who have been rung, and gave the reason: the form is how the district finds out about a person, and the whole reason she rang is that the district did not find out about him in seven weeks and should have. Her own cost, in a corridor, to nobody: she has rung one of about forty-one people since the Tuesday of week thirty-eight and did it once, in nine seconds, and she is going back on the Tuesday to try to do the other forty, and she has no rule for ringing forty-one people in a week and has not asked anybody for one, **and nobody is going to stop her and nobody is going to help her either and that is the difference and she has known it for a term and a half.** **He is not thanked, not found, and not going to be comforted, and the leaflet is still on his table and it is still about a practice and not about a man.**

### Chapter 0169 — "Eleven Slips In A Tray"

**The Wednesday morning of week fifty-three, from about half past ten, in the reading room in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 172, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is a tray with eleven slips in it in three weeks, none of them about a box, and a foreman of fifty-eight with one line of a slip written and three of them blank.** A counter with a bell, four drawers with about four hundred cards in them, a trolley with a high shelf, and a woman of forty-one who is in that room on the Monday, the Wednesday and the Friday, in the building since she was ten and in the room nine. **She counted the tray in about four minutes and had not counted it in nine years, and said the number out loud and did not tell him the number until she had decided to: eleven slips in about three weeks, and not one of them is about a box, against about forty people in nine years who have asked to see a binder.** Her position, out loud and unasked: in nine years nobody has ever come in and asked her a question that was not about a box, in about three weeks four people have, she has answered all four, she has not got a rule for answering a question, **and a rule that holds when eleven people want something it was not built for is still the same rule.** **A foreman of fifty-eight came in at about half past ten with one line of a slip filled in and three of them left, and gave both of his reasons: he wanted to see what a name at the bottom of a piece of paper in a public room felt like on paper once, and he has got a key to a basement and has not used it and would rather ask a person than open a door.** He said a plate on a door in one of his buildings says a number that is not the room, he has asked a company engineer twice and been told the list is the building's, the building's is a set of plans in a drawer on a hook in a basement in the fourth building where there has not been a person since the spring, **and he has a key to that building and has not gone down.** She told him there is nothing in that room about a building's own rooms, that it is what came out of this room and not what went into buildings, that she would not add a fifth thing to the sheet and that if he left it as it was he would have had a piece of paper and there is no tray for a piece of paper. He wrote the other three lines: *from when, never; to when, still; who is asking, Bryn Maw, gate, Meridian consolidation point.* **He was last in the prefab office off the Meridian corridor on the Thursday of week fifty-two, six days before this Wednesday.** **His cost, out loud, at a counter, in a public room: a name at the bottom of a piece of paper in a tray in a room anybody can walk into; he has said out loud in four buildings that about nine hundred doors on a company's list is not a thing that belongs to him and has got no receipt for any of it; this is the first piece of paper in this city with his name at the bottom of it about a door; and he is not taking it back, because a slip with his name on it is a thing somebody can point at in four years and say he asked, by somebody who has read about nine hundred plates and wants a name to go with them.** She named the other three slips because he asked and because she had decided to stop not saying: a man of sixty-ish who asked whether a thing can be safe because a lot of people have been given it, was told no, said thank you, did not give his name and was not asked for it; a woman who wanted to know about a card, who was told, and it was not written down, **that the difference between the two cards everybody is talking about is that one has a printer's shop name on the back of it and the other one has nothing**, and who said that was the first useful thing anybody has said to her in about four months; and a boy of nineteen who asked whether anybody had ever written down what a person said in a room like this and been believed, was told she did not know, and said that is the correct answer, and is not the boy on the till in a shop in the Quiet Quarter. **She will not count them again and will not say what the number means: in a month somebody is going to come in and say this room does a thing, she is not going to be the one who says it, and the only thing she has got to stop it with is that she is a woman who works three days a week and has said so out loud to a man with a carpenter's pencil in his ear.**

### Chapter 0170 — "When She Is Not There"

**The Tuesday and the Thursday of the week of fifty-four, in an office on a ground floor in another part of the city, in a reading room in Lattice Ward, and at ten at night in a repair shop. Load-book entry 173, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is half a room he was not in — a ring file of about a thousand sheets and a woman of thirty-six who turns every sheet over — and a tray in Lattice Ward with a slip in it written in the first person and signed by a repairer of twenty-two, and a woman of forty-one who works three days a week and was in the room on a Thursday.** **A ring file of about a thousand condition sheets in a cupboard, nineteen years old, and a woman of thirty-six who has done the sheets up for nine years and turns every sheet over because everybody does, and fourteen lines in pencil on the back of one of them.** She read all fourteen out, including the two in a stairwell in Saltmarket that a company plated in the year the stairwell was new and that are the worst of them because it is not anybody's mistake. **The correct action followed and it changes nothing: a line in a building book saying there are fourteen items, one sheet, the week of week fifty, no column, not actioned, and in about four years the pencil will have come off and there will be nothing on that page and that is not anybody's fault and not anybody being wrong. She is not going to ask for a second sheet and is not going to hand a woman of forty-six a job.** And then the name, said before the question was finished: **a man of twenty-two asked me a question in a corridor in the week of the forty-seventh that my sheet could not answer. His name is Marek Senn. He did not ask me to write anything down. That is your second time of asking and it is the last thing I am going to say about it.** She said who he is in one sentence, and **nobody thanked her and she is not on a distribution. The name is in two heads and there is no third account: it did not travel, he was not in that office, and he does not know it is anywhere.** **In the reading room, on a Thursday, which is not one of her three days, a woman of forty-one is behind the counter because she wanted to find out whether a person who is not asking her for a box would still find a person there, and there was no way of finding that out on a Wednesday because on a Wednesday she is there anyway.** **Marek Senn, twenty-two, a repairer, filled in a slip at about eleven and wrote across all four printed lines in one sentence in the first person in his own words and signed it with his name on it, and what was on it is his own object and not another man's: a card nobody has made, five things on it, four of them filled in and the fifth being the only one that matters, unfilled for a term and a half, and nobody ever having asked him to.** The four lines are about four things and the one thing did not fit them, **and the room took it and she was not nice about taking it and it is the same rule that has been in that room for nine years.** She gave the rule again in eleven seconds and said it is a rule and not a mood and it was a rule before she came, **and put both of her numbers on the counter unasked: about forty people have asked to see a binder in nine years and about two hundred people have been through that door since the spring, of whom about a hundred and sixty wanted a box, so that the traffic has gone up by about nine times what a year of this room used to be and nobody has told her why and she has not asked.** She also said that nobody had ever come in there and written one thing across four lines in the first person and signed it, and that she had been at that counter nine years and had not known until that morning that it was allowed. **What the room has for him is nothing: a card does not leave the room, a box does, a box needs a slip, he had a slip, and there is no card in that room for a card that has not been made, because there is nothing to write on the top line of it and no shelf to put it on, and that is not a refusal, that is what the room is.** What he has instead is a piece of paper with his name on it in a tray with eleven other pieces of paper, which is twelve in the tray, and in about four years somebody will be able to say a man of twenty-two came in on the Thursday of the week of fifty-four and asked, which has never been able to be said about that room before. **At about eleven twenty she said out loud that she was going to the post, which is in the same building and takes nine minutes, said it to him and to the bell and to nobody, and went out and left the door open the way she always does — and the whole of what that room does happens when she is standing behind the counter, and there is no form for it, no post, no minute and no notice, and it is a habit in a room with a counter and a person behind it, and a habit can be stopped by that person walking out.** The close reaches the volume's inventory and is no longer Chapter 160's close, which a review found to be a copy of it: a jacket on a chair with a corner of card in the inside pocket that has not left a room in a term and a half and the original in the fourth pocket of the tool roll with the fifth line still blank; a name in a box in pencil on a counter four miles away that cannot come out; about nine hundred doors with a company's service number off a room list nobody owns, and the drawing that would settle it in a drawer nobody has stood in front of since the spring, and a foreman of fifty-eight carrying a key he has told a stranger he has not turned; the fourth column of a register that has not had a person's name in it in fourteen years and now has an opinion about it in two heads; a tin and a book at forty-four lines with a hole in line forty-four and a fortieth line three years old and nobody has called and about eleven people have been told yes; sixteen Fridays on the back of a card in a wallet and in his own head; a man of fifty-one who has been rung in nine seconds and it was not a rescue; a town with four letters in it with a counter in it and a woman who will not say a name; and about eleven people a week still going to be taught to wait, and a room off a street standing open with a bell on its counter and nobody behind it.

### The measured figures for Movement II, reproducible from the ten files with the strict matchers printed in `workspace/volume-04/batch-0002/SUMMARY.md`

**29,994 words. Raw instances of *about*: 333, 11.10 per thousand. True hedges: 206, 6.87 per thousand, against the 6.91 this batch inherited and the 6.80 Volume 03's last batch finished in. Bold spans, strict matcher: 235, 7.83 per thousand, worst chapter 9.22**, and **the count excludes the fourteen `---` rule markers, which a whitespace-split counter reads as words**, against ceilings of 10.75 and 12.4. The matcher was verified against Volume 03 batch 0005's published line and against Movement I's published line before a word of Chapter 161 was drafted, and returns all three of their published counts exactly. **Chapters 165 and 167 are the batch's two lows on the true hedge at 3.93 and 3.94 and Chapter 169 its high at 10.14, all structural; Chapter 168 is the batch's worst on the raw *about* at 14.56 against a batch figure of 11.10, and that is the texture of three people speaking in the phrases the chapter is made of.** **All of these were re-measured in the review repair pass, which found the word counter was counting a `---` rule marker as a word and which moved Chapters 167, 169 and 170. The figures this block was first written with — 29,223 words, 312 raw, 185 true, 233 bold, a low of 2.24 and a high of 8.88 — are superseded and are not to be quoted.** Every line carries an even number of `**` and an even number of `"`; there is no `* * *` marker, no month-name, month-date, day-date, year or day number anywhere; there is no duplicated paragraph at an eighty-character threshold; and the word *pattern* appears nowhere, and so do the Crown Clause, Iona, Sorn, the Solo Seal, Nell, the Quiet House in the narrator's judgement, and *variant*.

### The two things a later writer must not undo in this block

1. **The Exchange's book is at forty-four lines, has not been opened for two sittings, and does not go to forty-five in this volume.** The reason is a rule and not a mood and it is canon: a book of what a caller said cannot be written by the people who did not call. **A Volume 04 writer may not open that book before the resolution and may not have a caller sign.**
2. **Both relationship beats are paid, in Chapter 163, in one corridor and one reported Sunday.** **Milestone 3 is Chapters 166 and 167 in the outline, is a public disagreement about method, and IS NOT DELIVERED and belongs to Chapter 171. A Chapter 171 writer must find it unwritten and must not mistake the standing problem in Chapter 167 for it.**
